ChooseFI has partnered with CardRatings for our coverage of credit card products. ChooseFI and CardRatings may receive a commission from card issuers.

There is exactly one scenario where BNPL is financially rational: a planned, budgeted purchase at 0% interest where you would have paid the full price regardless. For example, you need a new dishwasher, you have the $800 in savings, and the retailer offers four interest-free payments. You keep your $800 invested for six more weeks while paying in installments. The benefit is tiny — a few dollars of investment returns — but the cost is genuinely zero if you make every payment on time.

Outside this narrow scenario, BNPL is almost always a worse choice than the alternatives.

The financial independence community is nearly unanimous: BNPL is a trap disguised as convenience. The entire BNPL business model depends on you spending more than you planned. If you were going to buy the item anyway and you have the cash, just buy it. If you do not have the cash, that is your budget telling you something important.

The question to ask before any BNPL checkout: "Would I buy this right now if I had to pay the full price in cash today?" If the answer is no, BNPL is not helping you afford something — it is helping you overspend. If the answer is yes, just pay cash and skip the installment tracking, the payment reminders, and the risk of a missed payment damaging your credit.

For those building toward financial independence, every dollar matters. The 30-50% spending increase that BNPL encourages directly reduces your savings rate — the single most important variable in determining how quickly you reach FI.

The average ChooseFI listener saves $24,000+ with an expert student loan plan.

Trusted by Brad Barrett, Co-founder and Host, ChooseFI

Mortgage lenders are increasingly scrutinizing BNPL obligations during the underwriting process. Multiple active BNPL plans — even if interest-free — show up as recurring liabilities and can affect your debt-to-income ratio. If you are planning to apply for a mortgage or auto loan in the next 6-12 months, pay off all BNPL balances and avoid opening new ones. Lenders view fragmented installment obligations as a sign of cash flow stress, even if you have never missed a payment.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

Affirm is a legitimate, publicly traded company (AFRM on NASDAQ). It is safe in the sense that it will not steal your money or identity. However, being legitimate does not mean it is a good financial decision. Affirm's interest rates range from 0-36% APR depending on creditworthiness, and the company reports all payment activity to credit bureaus.

Increasingly, yes. Affirm reports to Experian and TransUnion. Afterpay reports to Equifax. Klarna financing plans report to all three bureaus. On-time payments may help slightly, but a single missed payment can significantly damage your score and remains on your report for up to seven years.

Afterpay charges an $8 late fee per missed payment, capped at 25% of the order value. After multiple missed payments, your account is suspended, the balance is sent to collections, and the delinquency is reported to Equifax. Collections accounts damage your credit score severely and remain for seven years.

Klarna's "Pay in 4" plan is interest-free with no late fees, but your account may be suspended for missed payments. Klarna's longer-term financing plans charge 7.99-33.99% APR, involve a hard credit pull, and report to all three credit bureaus. Read the terms carefully — the word "Klarna" covers very different products.

It is possible but unlikely for most people. Research consistently shows that BNPL increases spending by 30-50% per transaction. If you have the discipline to use BNPL only on planned, budgeted purchases at 0% interest — and never miss a payment — the financial harm is minimal. But for most users, the behavioral cost exceeds any convenience benefit.

Because it works — for the store. Merchants pay BNPL providers 4-8% of each transaction (vs 2-3% for credit cards) because BNPL increases average order values by 30-50% and reduces cart abandonment by 20-30%. The merchant is paying for you to spend more. That should tell you who this arrangement benefits.

Buy Now Pay Later is a marketing innovation designed to make you spend more — and the data confirms it works. BNPL users spend 30-50% more per transaction, 43% miss payments, and the credit reporting implications are growing. The only responsible use case is a planned, budgeted purchase at 0% interest where you already have the cash. For everyone else, especially those pursuing financial independence, BNPL is a detour in the wrong direction. Pay with cash, invest the difference, and let compound growth work for you instead of against you.

#10365. Markdown to PDF in 2026: No-Install Methods and the Rendering Problems Nobody Warns You About

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Converting Markdown to PDF is easy until the document has real content: long code blocks, wide tables, images and non-Latin text are where most tools fall apart.

Pandoc with LaTeX is still the most powerful route, but it needs a large install and trips over Unicode and emoji unless you switch engines and fonts.

Browser-based approaches (print CSS, Chromium-powered converters, in-browser tools) avoid the install and give you HTML-quality rendering, as long as you control page breaks.

A handful of print CSS rules (@page, break-inside: avoid, break-after: avoid) fix the majority of ugly PDFs, whichever tool you use.

Choose the tool by the document: a quick README export, a long technical report and a multilingual document each have a different best option.

I write almost everything in Markdown: READMEs, release notes, client handovers, meeting notes. And at least once a week, someone asks for "the PDF version". Every time, the conversion looks fine on the first page and falls apart somewhere around page three: a code block split in half, a table running off the edge, an emoji replaced by an empty box.

SitePoint covered the classic route back in 2015 with Creating PDFs from Markdown with Pandoc and LaTeX. That approach still works. But a lot has changed since then, and today there are several ways to get a clean PDF without installing a TeX distribution. This article compares them, then goes through the rendering problems you'll actually hit, with a fix for each.

Pandoc is still the Swiss Army knife. The simplest command is:

By default, that goes through LaTeX, which means you need a TeX distribution installed (several gigabytes for a full one). The output is beautiful for prose and math, but LaTeX has strong opinions about layout, and customizing it means writing LaTeX.

Pandoc can also render through an HTML engine instead, which lets you style the PDF with CSS:

Best for: long documents, academic writing, math, automated pipelines.

Watch out for: install size, Unicode handling with the default engine (more on that below).

Render the Markdown to HTML (any previewer, a static site, a GitHub page) and print it to PDF. It's free and already installed everywhere, but you inherit whatever the page looks like on screen: navigation bars, sidebars, and page breaks wherever they happen to fall.

Best for: quick, one-off exports of short documents.

Watch out for: zero control over page breaks unless the page ships print CSS.

Extensions like Markdown PDF for VS Code, or CLI tools like md-to-pdf, render your Markdown to HTML and print it with a headless Chromium:

You get the same rendering quality as a browser, plus a config file for CSS, margins, headers and footers. The trade-off is a Node install and a downloaded Chromium.

Best for: developers who live in their editor or want CI-friendly exports.

Watch out for: the first run downloads a browser, and print CSS is still your job.

The newest option is tools that do the whole conversion inside the browser tab: you paste or drop the .md file, preview the actual pages and download the PDF. The good ones don't upload your file anywhere, which matters for client documents and internal notes.

Best for: people who don't want to install anything, non-developers on your team, private documents.

Watch out for: many online converters do upload your file to a server; check before you paste anything confidential.

Whichever method you pick, the same handful of problems show up as soon as the document is longer than a page. Most of them are fixed with print CSS, which works in Pandoc's HTML engines, Chromium-based tools and any HTML you print from a browser.

A 20-line function cut after line 3, with the rest on the next page, is the most common complaint. The fix is to ask the renderer not to break inside the block:

One caveat: a block longer than a full page has to break. For those, it's better to let it break cleanly than to push it to a new page and leave a huge gap.

On screen, a long line in a code block scrolls horizontally. On paper, there's no scrollbar, so it's simply cut off. Wrap long lines in print:

Tables with many columns overflow the page or get squeezed until every cell is one word per line. Three things help: a smaller font size for tables, a landscape page for the worst offenders, and avoiding breaks in the middle of short tables:

If a table genuinely needs eight columns, consider whether it should be a table in the PDF at all, or an appendix in landscape.

A heading alone at the bottom of a page, with its content on the next one, looks broken. This rule keeps a heading attached to what follows it:

Support for break-after: avoid varies between rendering engines, so test it with yours; when it isn't honored, a manual page break before the heading is the reliable fallback.

Markdown has no syntax for a page break. The usual trick is a raw HTML element that print CSS turns into a break:

Some converters also recognize a comment like , which has the advantage of staying invisible when the same file is rendered on GitHub.

Images are inserted at their natural size, so a 4000-pixel screenshot spills off the page. Cap them:

For blurry images, the problem is usually the opposite: a small screenshot stretched to page width. Export screenshots at 2x if they're going into a PDF.

This one is specific to the LaTeX route. With the default pdflatex engine, Unicode characters outside its supported set produce errors or simply vanish. Switch to XeLaTeX or LuaLaTeX and give it a font that actually contains the characters:

Emoji are harder still in LaTeX and often need a dedicated package. HTML-based renderers handle both far better, because they use the operating system's font fallback just like a web page does.

Internal links ([see setup](#setup)) and external links should stay clickable. HTML-based renderers usually keep both. If yours doesn't, check that headings get IDs (most Markdown parsers add them) and that the PDF engine preserves link annotations.

Put together, these rules make a solid starting point for any HTML-based route:

Save it as print.css and pass it to Pandoc (--css=print.css with an HTML engine), to md-to-pdf through its config, or include it in the HTML you print from the browser.

My rule of thumb after a lot of trial and error:

A quick README or notes export, no install, private content: an in-browser converter. Full disclosure: this need is why I built the free Markdown to PDF converter on AwesomeToolkit, and it's what I use for this case now. It renders real A4 or Letter pages as you type, has an option to keep code blocks and tables whole, adds page numbers and a table of contents, and the conversion happens in the browser, so the file isn't uploaded anywhere.

Documentation in a repo, exported in CI: md-to-pdf or Pandoc with an HTML engine, plus the stylesheet above.

Long reports, math, academic formatting: Pandoc with XeLaTeX, using a font that covers every script in the document.

Something you'll print once and forget: the browser's Print to PDF is fine.

Whatever you choose, the biggest quality jump comes from the print rules, not the tool. Ten lines of CSS turn "it looks fine until page three" into a PDF you can actually send.

Yes. Your browser's Print to PDF works on any rendered Markdown, and in-browser converters do the whole conversion in a tab. Check that the converter processes files locally if the content is private.

The default pdflatex engine has limited Unicode support. Use --pdf-engine=xelatex or lualatex and set a font that contains the characters with -V mainfont= (and -V CJKmainfont= for CJK text).

Markdown has no native syntax for it. Insert

where you want the break, or use the page-break comment your converter supports.

Add pre { break-inside: avoid; } to your print CSS. Blocks longer than a page will still break, which is expected.

With HTML-based renderers, usually yes, both external links and links to headings inside the document. Test internal links in particular, since they depend on headings having IDs.

#10363. Samsung fans continue to hate on Galaxy S27 Ultra redesign

An alleged new video shows Galaxy S27 dummy units that look like the latest leaks, and Samsung fans aren’t having it.

At this point, it’s anyone’s best guess as to what the Galaxy S27 series looks like, but it’s seeming surer and surer that Samsung will be giving the series a big redesign this coming year. Chris Franklin, a writer at SammyGuru, posted an unboxing video of the Galaxy S27 on X, citing that there’s a possibility the unboxing is AI-created — there are some eyebrow-raising reflections here, but it’s hard to tell for certain. The design looks similar to the “confirmed” renders that leaked a few days ago, although there are some subtle differences.

I don't think much about the design of phones, they're essentially all the same, especially when considering you're looking at the front 90% of the time, but I've always found the Galaxy's vertical camera layout to be the least attractive of any of the major phones. I think this looks much better.

Unsurprisingly, another leak from earlier in the month has a similar look but, again, with subtle differences. So far, all the major leaks in the past few months are pointing to Samsung outfitting the Galaxy S27 series with a raised camera island that looks extremely similar to Apple’s design on the iPhone 17 Pro and 18 Pro series.

Samsung isn’t alone here. The Xiaomi 18 series and Honor Magic 9 Pro series both have eerily similar designs, and many other 2026 and 2027 phones have followed suit. The most obvious problem with Samsung’s potential design (and, by extension, Apple’s) is the raised camera lenses, which protrude above the raised camera island. Xiaomi’s island is flat, following Google’s idea that a camera bar should protect the lenses and also keep the phone from wobbling or feeling off-balance.

Drama has already taken off on X (formerly Twitter), where Noah Cat reposted the video, and it seems Samsung fans aren’t happy. Samsung has needed to change up the vertical camera bar for years, as it has regularly gotten in the way of Qi2 and MagSafe magnetic accessories. This new design might help that, but it seems fans wanted Samsung to return to the Galaxy S10 horizontal camera bar days, not this new iPhone-esque raised island.

An inspired developer has shoehorned a language model onto a floppy disk created especially for 486DX and Pentium PC users running DOS. Please note that WollyMonjac’s project isn’t a large language model (LLM). Though it shares the transformer model architecture of its much bigger siblings, Tiny 2.1 is humbly described as “a tiny question-answering transformer written in Turbo C 2.0.”

There isn’t much room for modern software on a floppy disk, with a typical PC formatted disk in the 486 era boasting just shy of 1,475,000 bytes of data capacity. Tiny 2.1 makes the best use of this limited headroom as possible, fitting in the following:

Your creaky old PC will need about 540KB of free conventional memory to run Tiny 2.1. If you do test it, we are sure you will appreciate the clean UI with its conversation history pane, neural monitor panel, and Ask Tiny prompt window. As all DOS programs should, there’s a range of F-key shortcuts highlighted within the UI. Perhaps the retro-futuristic cherry on the cake, though, is the PC-speaker robot voice which can be used to speak the responses.

We’ve already indicated that the dev seems rather humble about this DOS program. The GitHub repository says it was “Written by Fable 5.2 and trained by Opus 5.5.” Monjac also wryly admits that outside of this language model’s training, “it makes things up confidently.” We have heard less about LLM hallucinations in recent months, but here it feels almost like a fun feature.

Should any of Tiny 2.1’s knowledge gaps irk you, WollyMonjac has included a training folder in the MIT-licensed distribution. A dedicated user could probably build up a capable question-answer transformer tailored to their favorite topic(s).

Nowadays it is pretty easy to get old software to run on your shiny new hardware, one way or another. It isn’t entirely a one-way street, though; getting modern apps or games to run on hardware from decades earlier is an exciting development trend we are seeing established. In addition to the headlining Tiny 2.1, we’ve reported on similarly incongruous new-meets-old software projects like the ChatGPT client for DOS, or Minecraft running on a Game Boy, creating images on the C64 with generative AI, or playing Doom on an Atari ST.

#10357. What Is the Best UI design Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com.

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

BackstopJS is a free, open-source visual regression testing tool released under the MIT license. You list the URLs, viewports, and CSS selectors to capture in a config file, and BackstopJS renders them in a headless browser through Playwright or Puppeteer, then compares the results against approved reference screenshots. The core workflow runs on three commands: backstop init, backstop test, and backstop approve.

Strengths: Completely free, with no screenshot limits. A built-in browser report shows reference and test images side by side with a visual diff inspector, and changes can be approved from there. Optional Docker rendering keeps screenshots consistent between laptops and CI; Playwright adds Firefox and WebKit, and click, hover, and keypress selectors capture interactive states. JUnit reports plug into existing CI pipelines.

Trade-offs: BackstopJS is self-hosted, so there's no cloud dashboard, shared team review, or pull request status checks out of the box. Reference screenshots are usually committed to your repository, and every page or component state you want to test needs its own URL and scenario in the config. Someone on the team also has to own the setup, CI integration, and maintenance.

Best for: Developers and small teams with no budget who are comfortable running and maintaining their own visual regression setup.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, self-host BackstopJS, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic or Percy.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, For visual testing, Chromatic and Percy both offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month, and BackstopJS is free to self-host.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10356. Microsoft is shutting down the ‘To Do’ app that replaced Wunderlist, with no real replacement

Microsoft announced this week that its To Do app is shutting down soon, taking the legacy of Wunderlist with it.

Microsoft is shutting down its To Do app at the end of June 2027, with focus moving to the newer Planner app. The company explains:

As we bring personal task management into Planner, we will phase out To Do. The To Do apps will retire on June 30, 2027. Connections that other apps use to access To Do tasks will continue beyond that date, and we’ll provide separate notice before those connections are retired. We’re also updating existing task integrations across Microsoft 365 to connect with Planner, so you’ll be able to create or manage tasks in the Microsoft experiences you already use. Although To Do tasks are already visible in Planner, they will not be migrated to the new private plan. Customers using personal Microsoft accounts will need to choose another app. Our To Do transition guidance  will help you understand what’s changing and plan your next steps.

To Do was built on the foundation of Wunderlist, a wildly popular to do list app that Microsoft purchased in 2015. Wunderlist later shut down in 2020.

Microsoft’s shuffle, notably, leaves general consumers without a replacement app. To Do was available to anyone with a Microsoft account, where Planner is available only to enterprise/business users. So, for long-time Wunderlist users who followed Microsoft migration, this is the end of the road and it’s officially time to move to another app.

Hopping around the electricity grid, recharging on power lines, NOMADs don’t need to come home.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Tech startup Nomadic showcased its NOMAD drones this week alongside an announcement that it had raised $6 million in seed funding. These quadcopter drones have a unique skill hinted at by their name: they can basically fly forever without returning to base. NOMADs recharge by perching on high-voltage power lines, a ubiquitous feature of most landscapes, before moving on to their next task.

We hadn’t heard anything about the NOMAD drones until now, but Nomadic says it has tested its quadcopters for over 20,000 hours on live lines in Germany, Norway, and the U.S. “NOMADs are drones that land on live high-voltage lines, charge from the line itself, and relocate across the grid to where measurements are needed,” summarizes the startup.

The reference to measurements along the power grid reveals the current purpose of NOMAD drones. They are deployed to monitor the high-voltage lines from which they leech their energy. This is a very useful task for power companies. Nomadic explains that while modern grid operators use weather models to decide how much power each line can carry, the qualities of the lines can be quite variable. Meanwhile, the weakest point in any line “sets the limit for the whole line,” says the startup.

Fixed sensors have started to become the accepted solution for line monitoring, but mobile drones are a tantalizing alternative. In comparison to fixed sensors, the NOMAD, which recharges inductively from the line itself, seems like an ideal solution. Normally, an investigative high-voltage power line check with a human operator would also require extensive planning, heavy machinery, and the line to lose power for a short service period. Nomadic claims that a NOMAD drone can look after around 25km of line, while fixed sensors are typically positioned from 1km to 5km apart.

In addition to the drone hardware, there’s some integrated software to make the best use of the highly configurable data you can harvest from your drone fleet. “Nomadic OS combines their data with weather and grid models, giving operators a real-time picture of what is physically happening on their lines.”

NOMAD drones are also surprisingly tough. Units have been tested in temperatures as low as minus 25 degrees Celsius, in strong winds, and on lines up to 420kV (European transmission grid typical maximum). With the new funding, Nomadic is hoping to expand its fleet and build with more partners. The startup is also looking for several new recruits, mostly in engineering roles, all based in Munich, Germany.

#10351. Lenders back SpaceX's $43 billion Nvidia GPU deal but worry the collateral could end up in orbit, making them impossible to reclaim if Musk defaults

Creditors want to ensure they could still sell the AI chips in case SpaceX defaults on its loan.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk is borrowing $43 billion to finance SpaceX's acquisition of Nvidia AI chips for its ambitious data center projects and is using the semiconductors as collateral. However, Ars Technica reports that lenders are concerned that SpaceX will use the chips for its planned orbital data center, making it difficult, if not impossible, for creditors to reclaim in case the company defaults on its loan. SpaceX said it will deploy the collateralized chips only on Earth, but it remains unclear whether this provision is included in the signed debt instrument.

SpaceX is working on building massive data centers on both Earth and in space. Musk has already revealed the company’s first-generation AI1 satellite, with a 120-kilowatt payload and a span that’s larger than a Boeing 747, while he’s also planning to build a $24-billion gas-powered AI supercomputer in Starbase, Texas. These projects are in addition to the Colossus 1 and Colossus 2 sites near the Tennessee-Mississippi border.

Despite being one of the richest people on Earth right now, Musk is relying on a financing deal to acquire the chips he needs to achieve his dream of building an AI LLM that will “assist in the pursuit of understanding.” The $43 billion “Project Saturn” loan is also not structured like a typical loan, so its investment-grade rating would remain unaffected. Instead, it’s built similarly to aircraft leasing agreements, in which a leasing company buys the jets and leases them to the airline that wants to use them. This saves the airline from having to put up a significant amount of cash to get the airplanes that it needs.

For this deal, the Saturn Funding Trust, a fully owned subsidiary of asset-backed lending company WHCO, would acquire the Nvidia GPUs for SpaceX, with the latter paying the former quarterly for the privilege of using the AI chips. The deal is also described as a “five-year triple-net, hell-or-high-water finance lease arrangement,” meaning SpaceX must pay the lease for five years, whether the chips break, become obsolete, or the project becomes unprofitable. It’s also responsible for all the expenses of operating the chips.

These terms are very lender-friendly, as SpaceX basically assumes all the risks while also keeping them off its books. Rating agency Moody’s also said in its report that the chips will be deployed in SpaceX’s U.S. data centers. Nevertheless, the company’s plan to blast data centers into space is making some lenders nervous, regardless of its Earth-bound plans and promises.

#10349. Expect Samsung’s good-value Galaxy devices to get more expensive, report says

Rising memory costs are hitting mid-range devices the hardest. The latest report suggests Samsung will increase the price of its Galaxy A device series by 19% next year.

The report comes from Korean news outlet SEDaily. An industry source has reportedly stated that Samsung plans to increase the price of its mid-range phones starting next year. More specifically, the Galaxy A series.

The range between price jumps varies across a reported 8 Galaxy A series devices, but the highest value difference nearly hits a notable 19% increase. The largest jump reaches 18.6% for the Galaxy A17, which could increase from $269 to $319. It would no longer sit in a sub-$300 spot and wouldn’t be much of a value buy anymore.

Again, these are phones praised for sitting at a convenient price point. Samsung’s Galaxy A devices are generally good value and offer a lot of the best One UI features for a price that doesn’t come close to the current flagship selling points.

This comes after a report came out last month indicating Samsung cut production of its Galaxy A devices by “approximately 20%.” Memory makes up more of the overall component cost in mid-range devices. So the cost of the overall device is more influenced by memory prices than in a flagship, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra. Even with a specced-out build, there’s more dispersion of costly components, rather than one expensive component like memory taking up the bulk of the production price.

That cut is estimated to be around 10 million units, and this latest report only furthers the claim that mid-range devices are too costly to make for Samsung.

The opposite is true for the Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, which reportedly got the green light for increased production prior to launch. Price suggestions average an increase of $200 for the Galaxy S27 and Galaxy S27 Plus. As for the high-spec Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, they start at $1,399 and $1,499, respectively.

There was already a massive disparity between those devices and the increasingly validated Galaxy A devices, and the gap is shrinking.

#10348. Build Live Video Chat With WebRTC: From First Call to Production Debugging

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Adding real-time video to a web app used to mean plugins, proprietary SDKs and a lot of money. Today every modern browser ships with WebRTC, a set of APIs that lets two browsers exchange audio, video and data directly. The catch is that WebRTC gives you building blocks, not a finished feature. This guide walks through those blocks in the order you actually need them, with the exact API calls you need, and then covers the production issues that tutorials usually skip.

A browser-to-browser call has four moving parts:

Start with the local preview. Browsers only allow camera access on HTTPS or localhost, so serve your page accordingly.

1.     Request a stream with navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia(), passing video constraints (an ideal width of 1280 and height of 720 is a sensible default) and audio constraints with echoCancellation and noiseSuppression enabled.

2.     Assign the returned MediaStream to the srcObject property of your local video element.

3.     Keep a reference to the stream, because you will add its tracks to the peer connection later.

Set the muted and playsinline attributes on the local video element. Without muted users hear themselves, and without playsinline iOS Safari opens the video full screen.

The peers need a way to find each other. A tiny Node.js server using the ws package is enough for development. Its whole job is to relay messages between the peers in a room:

1.     Create a WebSocket server (for example with the ws package) listening on its own port.

2.     Keep a map of room names to the set of sockets in each room.

3.     When a client sends a join message, store the room name on the socket and add it to that room's set.

4.     For every other message type, forward the raw message to the other sockets in the same room, and never echo it back to the sender.

5.     On disconnect, remove the socket from its room so stale peers do not receive messages.

In production you would add authentication, limit room size to two for one-to-one calls, and validate message types, but the shape stays the same.

Both sides create an RTCPeerConnection, add their local tracks and listen for the remote ones:

1.     Create a new RTCPeerConnection with an iceServers list that contains at least one STUN server.

2.     Loop over the local stream's tracks and call addTrack() for each one.

3.     In the ontrack handler, attach the first remote stream to your remote video element.

4.     In the onicecandidate handler, send every new candidate to the other peer through your signalling channel as a candidate message.

One peer creates an offer, the other answers, and both apply each other's descriptions. Candidates arrive asynchronously in the background.

1.     The caller runs createOffer(), applies it with setLocalDescription() and sends the description as an offer message.

2.     The callee applies the offer with setRemoteDescription(), runs createAnswer(), applies it locally and sends it back as an answer.

3.     The caller applies the answer with setRemoteDescription().

4.     Both sides pass every incoming candidate message to addIceCandidate().

If both peers might start a call at the same time, look up the "perfect negotiation" pattern on MDN. It assigns one side as polite and resolves offer collisions cleanly instead of leaving both peers stuck.

On a home network, STUN alone often works. On corporate Wi-Fi, mobile carriers and symmetric NATs it frequently does not, and the call silently fails to connect. A TURN server relays media when a direct path is impossible. The open-source coturn server is the usual choice.

In practice, your iceServers list should contain your own STUN URL plus a TURN URL with a username and credential, so the browser can fall back to the relay automatically when direct connectivity fails.

Generate short-lived TURN credentials on your backend rather than hard-coding them, and budget for bandwidth: relayed calls pass every frame through your server.

Users deny camera access, unplug headsets and switch from Wi-Fi to 4G mid-call. Catch NotAllowedError and show a clear explanation, listen for devicechange events, and use RTCRtpSender.replaceTrack() to swap cameras without renegotiating the whole call.

Listen to pc.onconnectionstatechange. When the state becomes disconnected, wait a few seconds before giving up, because it often recovers. On failed, call pc.restartIce() to try new network paths.

Use RTCRtpSender.setParameters() to cap the bitrate on weak connections, and read pc.getStats() to monitor packet loss and round-trip time. Showing a small "poor connection" indicator does more for perceived quality than any codec tweak.

Mesh calls, where every participant connects to every other, work for two or three people. Beyond that, upload bandwidth explodes. Group calls need a Selective Forwarding Unit (SFU) such as mediasoup, LiveKit or Janus, which receives each stream once and forwards it to the others.

Tutorials end at a working demo, but the fastest way to understand what production WebRTC looks like is to watch a real call from the inside. Chrome ships an internal diagnostics page that shows every peer connection the browser opens, and it works on any site that uses WebRTC.

1.     Open chrome://webrtc-internals in one tab and keep it open.

2.     In another tab, start a call on your own demo and find its RTCPeerConnection entry.

3.     Expand that entry and look at the ICE candidate pairs: you will see whether the call went direct (host or srflx) or through a TURN relay (relay).

4.     Open the stats graphs for the outbound video stream and watch the bitrate, frame rate and resolution adapt when you throttle the network in DevTools.

5.     Check the negotiated codec and how quickly the connection recovers when you switch Wi-Fi off and back on.

Once you can read your own call, repeat the exercise on a production service. Any browser-based product works, whether a meeting tool or a live video chat that pairs two visitors, because webrtc-internals shows the same data for every site. Expect differences from your demo: more candidate pairs, a relay candidate in the list and a bitrate that adapts faster. Seeing them makes the earlier sections concrete, from why TURN matters to how bandwidth estimation reacts to packet loss.

The one-to-one call above is the core of many products, from telehealth consultations and tutoring to customer support. The engineering challenges grow with the use case: random matching needs queueing and presence on the signalling server, public platforms need moderation and reporting, and global audiences need TURN servers in several regions.

Start with the minimal version, get it working across Chrome, Firefox and Safari on both desktop and mobile, and add TURN before real users arrive. Once the call is reliable, every other feature becomes a much easier problem.

#10342. AI agents design an entirely new CPU architecture from scratch, startup claims

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Unlock exclusive tools and insights for enthusiasts who want more.

Access Bench, Roadmaps, deep analysis and other exclusive tools.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

Go beyond the headlines with expert reporting on the hardware industry.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

Go beyond the headlines with expert reporting on the hardware industry.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Its lead shrinks to 3.83x on the 15 workloads without AI tuning.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Architect Labs, a Palo Alto startup, said in a research blog post that its AI systems discovered, designed, and verified Frostbite, a general-purpose CPU with a new instruction set “built from the ground up.” Across 25 workloads, a four-tile Frostbite cluster delivered 4.59 times the performance per watt of a similarly sized “silicon-proven” dual-issue, in-order CPU core. Those numbers come from a simulated 7nm design that has not yet been made in silicon, and the company says it can’t name the comparison core for legal reasons.

Conventional CPUs have hardware that “rediscovers, at runtime, which instructions actually depend on each other.” Multicore designs also need deep cache hierarchies and coherence protocols, which keep every core’s view of shared memory consistent, but scale poorly and cost area and power. Architect Labs’ alternative is what it calls “multithreading for free,” where the program itself exposes the parallel work, without threads, locks, or out-of-order hardware required for that discovery.

Frostbite’s programs are compiled to interaction nets, a model of computation from 1990 in which a program is a graph of nodes and computation happens by rewriting pairs of connected nodes, and the chip is designed around this distinct paradigm. Pairs of nodes being rewritten never overlap, so they can run in any order, or all at once, and arrive at the same result. Every value is also used exactly once, so data can stay in small local memories instead of large shared caches.

An example of the difference given by the team is a 5x5 chess-like board with five queens, placed so none can attack another. A typical program searches for solutions one board at a time. As an interaction net, the same search breaks into 3,183 small operations, and the longest chain that has to run in order is only 262 steps, so on average about 12 operations are ready to run at once at each step. Nothing about the program was rewritten to get there, as the parallel work was already intrinsic.

Each of the chip’s tiles, small identical processing units linked by an on-chip network, has its own small local memory. Four tiles, plus four L3 nodes, form the cluster that’s benchmarked against the conventional core for the performance claims, but the full layout has 16 tiles. The design reached 800 MHz in early synthesis but ended up at 500 MHz in the final layout. The cluster also averaged 8.77 times the comparison core’s performance per area, and the table below shows how its performance per watt varies by workload.

Frostbite performance per watt vs. comparison core

It may come as some surprise that the AI which built Frostbite started with what the company calls “zero architectural priors,” though the team first picked the workloads it had to do well on, and this sort of blank slatism may have its advantages in avoiding bias. Instead, the agents read papers and benchmarks, built Python models, and then selected one concept for focus. The original 141-page PDF output, which contained a programming model, runtime, and high-level blocks, was deleted after the first register-transfer level (RTL) design was generated, because “many of the numbers and estimates were well off what was physically possible.”

That PDF was only ever a starting point. Once the agents picked an architecture, they moved away from English — as it can’t get every detail right or catch every mistake, according to the team — and wrote the spec in the company’s own language, called ASL. ASL translates to Lean, a proof checker, so it can be machine-checked, which the company says stops a large class of bugs and cuts down on agents gaming their objectives. From this, the agents devised Arcmodel, a software model of the whole chip written in ASL, before any circuit design existed.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Simulating the RTL is slow, so the agents tested ideas on the model instead, which ran about 200 times faster. At first, the model’s power estimates were off by up to almost 250%, but an automated loop adjusted it until it matched the circuit design within just 5%.

From there, swarms of agents proposed changes in parallel, and each one was checked against the spec, tried in the fast model, built into the circuit design, and measured for power before being kept or dropped. This led to 58 ideas tried, with 37 kept. Separately, a behavior emerged that “was never specified by any human,” in what is known as work stealing: a tile with nothing to do can pull spare work from a neighbor or, in lieu of anything to do, go idle to save power.

Share of energy (one four-tile cluster, mean of 20 workloads, excluding clock distribution)

Fetching, decoding, and issuing instructions

Clock distribution (share of total energy, left out of the rows above)

This isn’t Architect Labs’ first design, as it showed off Redwood, an AI inference accelerator for robots and drones, in August. In that case, the company says its AI generated the RTL, verification, firmware, drivers, and kernels in under two weeks from a spec written by two human architects. Redwood still runs only on an FPGA and hasn’t been made in silicon, though the company says it is working toward having it built at TSMC.

Tendrils Compute, a possible rival based in Cambridge, U.K., raised funding earlier this month for a processor it says will have “thousands of cores operating in parallel.” It is also developing Vine, the same language Frostbite’s programs are written in.

Architect Labs says more benchmarks are coming, but they’re only part of the plan. It also wants an FPGA-hosted “software playground for anybody to run a program on Frostbite” along with a “preliminary operating system.” Eventually, it plans to release Frostbite’s RTL and instruction set.

In the meantime, the company plans to cover more workloads and compare “a broader set of CPUs,” including larger Frostbite mesh configurations against multicore versions of the comparison core with large caches. It also plans to test against “larger, wider, out-of-order CPUs,” with these comparisons due in the coming months on popular CPU benchmark suites such as SPEC and PARSEC.

#10339. Building Email Integrations: APIs, Webhooks, Automation, and Deliverability

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Email remains an important communication channel for web applications. From account verification and password resets to product announcements and customer notifications, many applications depend on email to keep users informed.

For developers, however, implementing email functionality involves more than connecting a service and sending a message. A reliable email system needs to handle authentication, dynamic content, delivery failures, user preferences, event tracking, and integrations with other application components.

Using an email service provider (ESP) can simplify this process. Most modern providers offer REST APIs, SMTP relays, templates, automation features, and reporting tools that developers can integrate into existing applications.

This guide explores the technical considerations involved in building email integrations, including API-based sending, webhook handling, automation workflows, and deliverability.

There are two common ways to connect an application to an email delivery service: SMTP and REST APIs.

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is a standard protocol used to transfer email messages between servers. Developers can connect an application to an SMTP server using credentials and configuration settings.

REST APIs provide another approach. Instead of communicating through an email server protocol, an application sends HTTP requests to an endpoint. The provider processes the request and returns a response.

Both methods can support transactional and application-generated emails, but their implementation differs.

An application already supports SMTP configuration.

Developers want a relatively straightforward integration.

Existing frameworks or libraries provide built-in SMTP support.

An application needs programmatic control over email operations.

Developers want structured responses and error handling.

Email functionality needs to integrate with other application services.

The system requires event tracking or additional API capabilities.

The right approach depends on the application's architecture, expected email volume, and integration requirements.

An API integration allows an application to trigger emails in response to specific events.

For example, an ecommerce application might send an order confirmation after a successful payment. A SaaS application could send an account verification message after registration.

A typical integration follows this process:

The backend prepares the recipient and message data.

The backend authenticates with the email provider.

The application records the response and handles any errors.

Consider a Node.js application that needs to trigger an email campaign through an API.

Some platforms, including Mailmodo, provide REST API functionality for triggering campaigns and integrating external applications with email workflows.

The following example demonstrates the general structure of an HTTP request using a campaign-triggering endpoint:

This example uses the documented Mailmodo trigger endpoint and request structure. The campaign ID and API key must be replaced with the values from the relevant account.

Notice that the API key is loaded from an environment variable rather than written directly into the source code.

This is an important security practice. Credentials should never be exposed in frontend JavaScript, public repositories, or client-side application bundles.

Not every email provider offers the same integration capabilities. Developers should evaluate providers based on their API documentation, authentication methods, supported endpoints, event notifications, and integration flexibility.

For example, Elastic Email provides a REST API that developers can use to send emails and manage different email operations from an application.

Its API uses HTTPS requests and API-key authentication. Developers can use the available endpoints to send messages, manage campaigns, retrieve information, and configure webhook notifications.

When evaluating an email API, consider these technical requirements:

Authentication: Check how API keys are generated, stored, restricted, and rotated.

Documentation: Look for clear endpoint descriptions, request examples, response formats, and error codes.

SDK availability: Official libraries can reduce the amount of integration code required.

Permission management: Separate credentials for sending emails and accessing account information can reduce unnecessary exposure.

API limitations: Review connection limits, request timeouts, rate limits, and account-level restrictions before deploying an integration.

A provider with extensive features is not necessarily the best option for every project. The important consideration is whether its API supports the application's actual requirements.

Sending an email is only one part of the communication process. Applications may also need to know what happens after a message is submitted.

A webhook allows an external service to send an HTTP notification to an application when a particular event occurs.

Depending on the provider, events may include:

For example, an application could receive a delivery-failure notification and update the corresponding email record in its database.

A basic Express.js webhook endpoint might look like this:

This is a simplified example rather than a complete provider-specific webhook implementation. A production endpoint should validate the provider's signature or other authentication mechanism and follow its documented payload structure.

It should also handle duplicate events safely. Webhook providers may retry notifications when an application does not respond successfully.

An idempotent event handler prevents the same notification from creating duplicate database updates or triggering repeated actions.

Email automation connects application events with predefined communication sequences.

Instead of manually sending every message, developers can create workflows that respond to user activity.

Consider a SaaS application with the following registration process:

If the user does not complete onboarding, a follow-up message may be triggered.

A simplified workflow can be represented as:

In a production application, email delivery should generally be handled through a background job queue rather than blocking the registration request.

A queue also makes it easier to retry temporary failures, manage sending volume, and prevent email delays from affecting the main application.

Developers should consider tools such as Redis-backed queues or framework-specific job processors when building larger systems.

Automation platforms can also help manage more complex journeys. For instance, a developer can connect application events to an external email workflow instead of implementing every communication sequence independently.

The key is to keep business logic in the application while using the email service to manage message delivery and relevant campaign operations.

A successful API response does not necessarily mean that an email has reached the recipient's inbox.

Email deliverability depends on several technical factors, including domain authentication, sender reputation, message quality, and recipient engagement.

Developers should pay attention to the following configurations.

Sender Policy Framework identifies the mail servers authorized to send messages on behalf of a domain.

A correctly configured SPF record helps receiving servers evaluate whether an email originates from an authorized source.

DomainKeys Identified Mail adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages. Receiving servers can use the corresponding public key in DNS to verify the signature.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance builds on SPF and DKIM. It allows domain owners to publish policies for handling messages that fail authentication checks and receive reports about authentication activity.

These three mechanisms should be configured carefully, especially when an application sends emails through multiple providers.

Email systems generate useful operational data. However, developers should avoid treating every available metric as an accurate measure of user engagement.

More reliable application-level signals can include:

These events can be connected with email campaign data to understand how communication affects application activity.

Developers should also maintain a clear separation between email delivery records and user account records. This makes it easier to investigate delivery issues without unnecessarily modifying customer data.

Before deploying an email integration, developers should review the following areas:

API security: Store credentials in environment variables or a dedicated secrets manager.

Domain authentication: Configure SPF, DKIM, and DMARC.

Error handling: Account for failed requests, invalid recipients, and temporary provider errors.

Background processing: Use queues for asynchronous email delivery.

Webhook validation: Verify incoming notifications and handle duplicate events.

Monitoring: Track delivery failures, bounce rates, and application-level outcomes.

Testing: Test email templates across different clients and devices before production deployment.

These practices help create an email system that is easier to maintain, troubleshoot, and scale.

Email integration is an important part of modern application development, but a reliable implementation requires more than connecting an SMTP server or calling an API endpoint.

Developers need to consider authentication, asynchronous processing, webhook security, automation logic, domain configuration, and event monitoring.

REST APIs are useful for applications that need programmatic control over email operations, while SMTP remains a practical option for systems that already support standard mail-server connections. Webhooks add another layer by allowing applications to respond to delivery events and update their internal records.

By choosing an integration method that fits the application architecture and following sound security and deliverability practices, developers can build email functionality that supports both immediate communication needs and long-term application growth.

What's your FI number? Calculate it in 60 seconds

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

"Is Social Security taxable?" is one of the most common tax questions retirees ask — and the answer frustrates almost everyone: it depends. Depending on your total income, anywhere from 0% to 85% of your Social Security benefits can be subject to federal income tax. The tax was introduced in 1983, originally affecting about 10% of recipients. Today, because the income thresholds were never indexed for inflation, roughly 56% of Social Security recipients pay tax on their benefits.

The good news: even in the worst case, you'll never pay tax on 100% of your Social Security. The bad news: the formula for calculating the taxable amount is one of the most convoluted in the entire tax code. In this guide, we'll break it down into plain English, show you exactly how to calculate what you owe, and — most importantly — share strategies the FI community uses to reduce or eliminate the tax entirely.

The IRS uses a special formula called "combined income" (also called "provisional income") to determine how much of your Social Security benefits are taxable. Here's the formula:

Combined Income = Adjusted Gross Income (AGI) + Nontaxable Interest + Half of Social Security Benefits

The resulting number determines which taxation tier you fall into. Notice that the thresholds haven't changed since 1993 — they are not adjusted for inflation, which means more retirees cross into taxable territory every year.

Important: "Up to 85% taxed" does not mean you pay an 85% tax rate. It means 85% of your benefits are added to your taxable income and taxed at your ordinary income rate. If you're in the 22% bracket and 85% of your $30,000 benefit is taxable, you'd owe 22% on $25,500 — roughly $5,610.

This is the most common Social Security tax confusion. When the IRS says "up to 85% of benefits are taxable," it means 85% of your benefit amount gets added to your taxable income and taxed at your regular rate. If your marginal rate is 22% and 85% of your $30,000 benefit is taxable, you owe about $5,610 — not $25,500. The effective tax rate on your full benefit in this example is about 18.7%.

Let's walk through a real example. Meet Sarah: she's 67, single, retired, and receives $24,000 per year in Social Security benefits. She also has $18,000 in IRA withdrawals and $2,000 in municipal bond interest.

Step 2: Determine the Tier Sarah is single with combined income of $32,000, which falls between $25,000 and $34,000. She's in the 50% tier — up to 50% of her benefits may be taxable.

Step 3: Calculate the Taxable Amount The actual calculation involves comparing two formulas and taking the lesser amount. For the 50% tier:

Sarah adds $3,500 to her other taxable income. At a 12% marginal rate, that's roughly $420 in additional federal tax on her Social Security.

Beyond federal taxes, 13 states also tax Social Security benefits to some degree as of 2026. Each state has its own thresholds and exemption amounts:

The remaining 37 states and Washington, D.C. do not tax Social Security benefits at all. If you're planning where to retire, this is a meaningful factor — especially if you have substantial SS benefits combined with other retirement income.

The FI community has identified several strategies that can significantly reduce the tax bite on Social Security benefits. The key insight: since taxation is based on combined income, controlling your income in retirement is the lever.

This is the single most powerful strategy. Roth IRA withdrawals are not included in the combined income formula. If you retire early (before Social Security age), you have a window of low-income years to convert Traditional IRA/401(k) money to Roth.

Example: Between ages 55 and 67, you convert $500,000 from your Traditional IRA to a Roth — paying tax at rates as low as 10-12%. When you claim Social Security at 67, your Roth withdrawals don't push you into a higher SS taxation tier. Instead of 85% of your benefits being taxable, you might pay tax on 0-50%.

The math is dramatic over a 25+ year retirement. Converting at 12% during early retirement to avoid 22%+ taxation on Social Security for decades produces enormous tax savings.

Every dollar of AGI pushes you toward the 50% or 85% taxation tiers. Strategic levers include:

Once you reach age 70.5, you can donate up to $105,000 per year directly from your IRA to a qualified charity. This satisfies your Required Minimum Distribution (RMD) without the distribution being included in your AGI. Lower AGI means lower combined income, which means less Social Security is taxable.

For charitably inclined retirees, QCDs are one of the most tax-efficient giving strategies available — they simultaneously reduce your income tax AND reduce the tax on your Social Security benefits.

Delaying Social Security from 62 to 70 increases your benefit by roughly 77%. But there's a secondary benefit: during the delay years, you have more room to do Roth conversions at low tax rates without Social Security benefits adding to your combined income. The combination of larger benefits and lower taxation on those benefits makes delay doubly powerful for tax optimization.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

If you retire before claiming Social Security, you have a golden window for Roth conversions. With little or no earned income and no SS benefits, you can convert Traditional IRA money to Roth at the lowest tax brackets of your lifetime. Every dollar you convert is a dollar that will never increase the tax on your Social Security benefits. This window closes permanently once you start claiming.

A step-by-step walkthrough for estimating what you owe.

List your AGI from all sources: IRA/401(k) withdrawals, pension income, wages, dividends, capital gains, rental income. Get your total Social Security benefit from your SSA-1099 form.

Pro tip: Don't forget nontaxable municipal bond interest — it counts in the combined income formula even though it's not on your 1040.

Add your AGI + nontaxable interest + half of your Social Security benefits. This "combined income" number determines your taxation tier.

Compare your combined income to the thresholds: Single — $25K (50% tier), $34K (85% tier). Married filing jointly — $32K (50% tier), $44K (85% tier).

For the 50% tier: take 50% of combined income above the lower threshold (capped at 50% of benefits). For the 85% tier: the calculation is more complex — use IRS Publication 915 worksheet or tax software.

Pro tip: Tax software handles this automatically. If doing it by hand, IRS Publication 915 has the complete worksheet.

The taxable portion of your Social Security gets added to your other taxable income and taxed at your marginal rate. Check if your state also taxes Social Security.

For single filers, Social Security becomes partially taxable when combined income exceeds $25,000 (50% tier) and up to 85% taxable above $34,000. For married filing jointly, the thresholds are $32,000 (50% tier) and $44,000 (85% tier). These thresholds have not changed since 1993 and are not indexed for inflation.

No. Social Security benefits are not earned income. They are classified as unearned income for tax purposes. This distinction matters for things like the Earned Income Tax Credit (EITC), IRA contribution eligibility, and the Social Security earnings test (which only applies to earned income like wages and self-employment).

No — and this is why Roth accounts are so powerful in retirement. Roth IRA withdrawals are not included in the combined income formula used to determine Social Security taxation. Converting Traditional IRA money to Roth before claiming Social Security can dramatically reduce the tax on your benefits for life.

Yes. File Form W-4V with the Social Security Administration to have federal income tax withheld from your benefits. You can choose 7%, 10%, 12%, or 22% withholding. This avoids the surprise of a large tax bill at filing time. State tax withholding varies by state.

Social Security benefits are included in the Modified Adjusted Gross Income (MAGI) calculation that determines Medicare Part B and Part D premium surcharges (IRMAA). High-income retirees can pay significantly more for Medicare — up to 3.4 times the standard premium. Managing MAGI in retirement affects both Social Security taxation and Medicare costs.

There have been numerous proposals to reform Social Security taxation, including indexing the thresholds for inflation, raising the taxable percentage above 85%, or eliminating the tax entirely. As of 2026, no changes have been enacted. The thresholds have remained unchanged since 1993, meaning inflation automatically pushes more retirees into taxable territory each year.

Yes, Social Security benefits can be taxable — up to 85% of your benefits may be added to your taxable income depending on your combined income. The thresholds are low ($25,000 single, $32,000 married) and haven't been adjusted for inflation since 1993, catching more retirees every year. The single most powerful strategy is Roth conversions during early retirement years before claiming Social Security. Every dollar in a Roth account is a dollar that won't push your Social Security into the taxable zone. Plan your retirement income sources deliberately, and you can reduce or eliminate this tax entirely.

#10336. The two best kids’ smartwatches are $50-$64 off ahead of Black Friday

Garmin and Bark are competing for your money this year, and these sales make it easy to get something fun that’ll keep your kids safe and in touch.

Many parents struggle with the idea of getting a smartphone for their kids. If you want your kids to stay in touch but not have access to all the garbage on the internet, a smartwatch is the best alternative. I’ve tested a lot of kids’ smartwatches over the years, and I can easily recommend the two in the picture above: the Bark Watch and the Garmin Bounce 2.

Both watches are on sale well ahead of Black Friday, too! The Bark Watch is $11 off at Amazon ($274 total) and $50 off at Bark, while the Garmin Bounce 2 is $50-$64 off (starting at $235) depending on the color you choose. The least expensive Garmin Bounce 2 is the blue color in the photo I took above, which my son loved.

Choosing between the two is easy enough. Garmin’s watch is the least complicated and comes in a slightly more durable case with fun colors. It’s slightly cheaper than Bark, but doesn’t have any health sensors on board. It mainly tracks steps, allows your kids to message or call you, and gives you geofencing and location tracking through a $9.99 per month LTE subscription plan. It’s got a 5ATM rating and a polycarbonate case, so it’s quite durable.

The Garmin Bounce 2 is preloaded with a few mini games and educational apps, plus the Garmin Jr. app has built-in unlockables and rewards parents can set for doing chores, completing schoolwork, etc. Battery life is roughly 2 days with normal use, but can be less if you’re in an area with poor LTE reception. The Garmin Bounce 2 is best for kids 10 and under.

The Bark Watch uses Samsung Galaxy Watch 8 hardware and a custom version of Wear OS. It’s a much more powerful watch and only gets a day of battery life because of it. This one feels like a midpoint between a kids’ watch and a standard Wear OS watch, as it has all the power of Wear OS but doesn’t feature the Google Play Store. It doesn’t have any games, social media, or browsers.

Since it uses Samsung’s hardware, it includes cutting-edge health sensors that your kids can use, plus great multitasking and a svelte, attractive design. Bark charges $15 per month for its plan, which includes a Bark App subscription for the whole family. I also use the Bark App on my son’s phone, which helps me monitor his usage and make sure he’s safe online. The extra $5 per month adds up, but it’s absolutely worth it if you want to add the Bark App to another phone or tablet.

The Bark Watch is fine enough for younger kids, but the design and lack of games feel more like an “older kids’ watch” to me, and my 12-year-old son agrees. Either way you go, these are fantastic choices that you’ll be happy with, especially with a nice little deal.
