'It was a very cool thing to do, no pun intended' says the electronics and 3D printing hobbyist.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A wall of 100 Noctua PC fans was built by maker Sasa Karanovic because "why not." There's a little more to it, though, as the Toronto-based electronics and 3D printing expert wanted to test the capabilities of his new open-source open-hardware OpenFAN XL fan controller. A video shows the setup and control of a 10x10 Noctua fan grid controlled via the 10 headers on the controller. A 50x2 setup was also shown working off the controller. Karanovic indicates that 100 fans was "no sweat" for the new controller, but it would max out at around 200 spinners in its current iteration.

The maker explains in the video that they had this demo idea a while back but recently acquired 100 fans through a combination of "a ton of luck and a good friend." As the new OpenFAN XL controller has 10 headers, the natural first step in controlling them all was to make a grid of 10 x 10 units. That's a lot of fans, but there's also 200 fan grills, 800 screws, and oodles of wiring, including tens of fan splitters.

So, the 100 fans aren't individually controllable, they are controlled in banks of 10 due to the 10 header channels. Thus, the granularity of control on offer here can only apply to 10 fans at the fewest in an array of 100 fans. The maker insisted that with 100 fans "our fan controller is not even breaking a sweat." Incidentally, the controller supports up to four integrated temperature probes to check if it is, actually, sweating.

Karanovic also sought to show the flexibility of his system by using wiring/splitters so that the first two headers controlled 50 fans each. If he'd had enough fans he could have theoretically expanded the cooling wall to 500 fans controlled by his OpenFan software in this way. But there are some other limits to the controller which means it tops out at around 200 fans.

In summary, this beastly cooling "contraption probably makes no sense," admitted the OpenFAN XL controller designer. However, he could have demonstrated it with some TrashBench-style shenanigans (or in an actual collab) by funneling a 100 fan-powered gust of air at a GPU, PC, laptop or so on.

If you can think of a use for the OpenFan XL, you can make one yourself or grab one from Karanovic Research for $2,355, currently discounted from $2,926 on pre-order, by following the link in the intro. The product retail page more seriously pitches the controller as a useful product for server or other high-power device builders.

#10298. Serving a Local LLM to Several Users: Ollama, LM Studio, llama.cpp and vLLM Benchmarked

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When one person chats with a local model, it barely matters which server you run. On one RTX 3090 with the same model file, Ollama, LM Studio and llama.cpp wrote text within about 10 percent of each other, because all three run llama.cpp underneath. That changes when your app sends several requests at once. With four requests, the best setup produced more than three times as much text as the worst, and the cause was a default setting, not the engine. With 32 requests, vLLM produced 2.5 times as much text as the best llama.cpp setup I tested.

This article puts those numbers side by side and shows which setting to change for your app.

I ran every test on one machine. The full write-ups, with every command and the raw results, are on homebrewserver.club.

Ollama, LM Studio and llama.cpp loaded the exact same GGUF files, checked by SHA-256. vLLM cannot run GGUF files well, so it loaded a 4-bit AWQ file of the same model (9.1 GB). Answer quality was not compared.

The tests ran between 8 and 16 November 2026, and speeds on this machine varied by a few percent from one day to the next. Treat a gap under about 5% as a tie. All of this is one consumer GPU and one model family, so read the numbers as a guide to how the servers behave, not as a ranking for your hardware.

This is the short prompt with a 512-token answer.

Ollama and LM Studio both start llama.cpp's llama-server program for each model they load. Ollama has done this since version 0.30, and a comment in its source code says so. On Linux, LM Studio's CUDA runtime is a folder with llama.cpp libraries and a llama-server binary.

Ollama logs the command it starts when you set OLLAMA_DEBUG=1. When llama.cpp was started by hand with that exact command, in a run next to Ollama, its speed matched Ollama's within 1%. So Ollama adds no speed cost of its own. LM Studio was 5 to 10% slower in this test. The test did not separate whether that came from its llama.cpp build or its other start options.

For one user, the gap between Ollama and LM Studio was about 0.4 s on a 512-token answer from the 9B model. You would notice it in long answers, and hardly in a chat window.

A web app rarely sends one request at a time. A chat page, a background summary job and an agent can all call the same model at the same moment. The next test sent four requests at once, each asking for 256 tokens, and measured the total output per second.

A slot is one request that the server works on at the same time as others. llama-server sets the number of slots with -np (short for --parallel), and Ollama and LM Studio pass their own setting through to it.

With one slot, the server answers the four requests one after another, so the total stays at the speed of a single request. That is Ollama's default. With four slots, the server puts the four requests through the model together, and the total rose to more than twice that of one slot. That is the default in LM Studio and llama.cpp. vLLM was the fastest at four requests, but it was tested only with the 9B model. Its lead grows with more requests, as the section after next shows.

For your app, this means that the fourth user of a default Ollama server waits for the first three answers to finish before their own answer starts.

The obvious fix is OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=4. It worked for the 9B model, though in one of three rounds Ollama still answered at the speed of a single request. With the 27B model, it made Ollama slower than its default.

The cause is how each server sizes the context, the memory that holds each request's prompt and answer. Ollama picks the context from the GPU's memory: 32,768 tokens on a 24 GB card, according to its docs. With four slots, it gives each slot its own 32,768 tokens, so it started llama-server with a 131,072-token context. The Ollama FAQ says the memory needed grows with the number of parallel requests multiplied by the context length.

That context did not fit next to the 27B model in 24 GB. ollama ps showed 16% of the model on the CPU, and the total speed fell to 33.1 tokens per second.

LM Studio and llama.cpp handle it differently. Their four slots share one pool of context:

The price of LM Studio's default is a short context. Four requests sharing 8,192 tokens leave little room for long documents or a long chat history. If your app sends long prompts, load the model with a larger context.

In Ollama, setting a smaller context with OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH next to OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL should avoid the spill to the CPU. The test did not try this, so measure it on your own card.

The last test went further, from 1 to 32 requests at once, with the 9B model. It compared vLLM, a server built to serve one model to many users, with llama.cpp at its defaults and with more slots. Ollama and LM Studio were tested only up to four requests, so they are not in this table.

With its defaults, llama-server chose four slots by itself, so it worked on four requests and queued the rest. Its total stayed near 330 tokens per second from 4 requests to 32. Setting -np 16 or -np 32 roughly doubled the total at 16 requests.

vLLM's lead came from each request running faster inside a large batch. At 32 requests, each vLLM request generated about 61 tokens per second, against about 27 on llama.cpp with 32 slots. vLLM's README names the two techniques behind this: PagedAttention, which stores each request's working memory in small blocks, and continuous batching, which adds new requests to the batch the GPU is already working on.

Total output tells you how much work the server does. The wait for the first word tells you how the app feels to the user behind the last request:

For a web app, that is 22.5 seconds of spinner before any text appears. Long prompts make every wait longer, on both servers. With eight requests of about 4,120 tokens each, the last request waited 7.9 s on vLLM and 8.8 s on llama.cpp with 32 slots.

If you set the slots yourself, the llama-server README lists three options that work together:

If you run an older llama.cpp, update first. Four requests at once ran 14 to 22% faster on v1.0.0 than on the build from five days earlier, while one request stayed the same. The most likely reason is a new CUDA kernel in v1.0 for steps of 2 to 24 tokens on RTX 30-series and newer cards.

vLLM's throughput comes with two costs that matter on a shared machine.

It reserves most of the GPU. By default, vLLM takes 92% of the card's memory before it loads anything, and fills what the model does not need with cache. With a desktop and a browser holding about 2 GiB, vLLM refused to start:

Setting --gpu-memory-utilization 0.6 alone was not enough with this model, because the default limit of 256 sequences needed more cache than fit. Adding --max-num-seqs 32 fixed it. With both settings, vLLM held 12.7 GiB and kept the same speeds. llama.cpp works the other way: it sizes its cache from -c and -np and never asked for more than 15.1 GiB in this test.

It starts slowly. llama-server answered requests 2 to 5 s after its container started. vLLM compiles the model before it serves anything. That took 72 s with its compile cache already on disk, and 173 s on the first start without it. Changing a vLLM argument triggered a fresh compile.

It also needs a different model file. vLLM loads Hugging Face safetensors, so a 4-bit model is a separate AWQ or GPTQ upload. The AWQ file here was 9.1 GB against 5.7 GB for the GGUF, because its recipe leaves some layers unquantized. Check the file size, not only "4-bit", when you size a model for your card.

All four servers have an OpenAI-compatible API under /v1, so the same client code works with each one. You change the base URL and the model name. These are the ports used in the tests:

In Node.js with the openai package, that looks like the sketch below. The measurements came from a Python test client, and this snippet was not part of them:

Qwen3.5 thinks before it answers unless you turn it off. The tests sent "chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": false} in the request body to vLLM and llama.cpp.

None of the servers asked for a password in these tests. llama-server also warned at start-up that its CORS rule allowed all origins. Bind the port to 127.0.0.1, as in the table, and set --api-key on llama.cpp or vLLM if anything else on your network can reach it.

Before you choose, count how many requests your app really sends at the same moment. Log the timestamps for a day, or count the parallel calls your agent makes per user action. If that number is one, any of these servers will do. If it is four or more, the slot setting will change your users' wait more than the choice of engine.

Not for one request with the same settings. Ollama runs llama.cpp's own server, and the two were within 1% when started with the same command. llama.cpp was faster with four requests at once, because it starts four slots by default and Ollama starts one.

Ollama gives each parallel request its own full context. With four slots on a 24 GB card, that was 131,072 tokens, which pushed 16% of a 27B model onto the CPU. A smaller context may avoid that, but this test did not measure it.

When many requests arrive together. At 32 requests, vLLM produced 2.5 times the output of llama.cpp with 32 slots and kept every first-word wait under a second. For one request at a time, llama.cpp was 5% faster and started in seconds instead of more than a minute.

Usually not. All four serve OpenAI-compatible endpoints under /v1, so you change the base URL and the model name.

Nintendo raised the price of the Switch 2 earlier this year in response to rising NAND and DRAM prices, but on Black Friday, you can wipe out that hike with a variety of bundles available on Amazon. The core deal is the Mario Kart World Switch 2 bundle, which includes the game, the console, and three months of Nintendo Switch Online. Amazon is extending that sale to a variety of bundles it has available. You can pick up the kit alongside a Switch 2 Pro Controller, the new Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake, or a microSD Express card with the same discount. Note that with the base Mario Kart World bundle, the game is included as a redeemable code, not a physical cartridge.

This bundle includes the Nintendo Switch 2 console, a code for Mario Kart World, and three months of a Nintendo Switch Online Individual membership.

As you can read about in our Nintendo Switch 2 review published last year, the console is a major upgrade over the original version. The heart of the handheld is a custom Nvidia SoC that includes eight Arm Cortex-A78C CPU cores alongside an Nvidia GPU with 1,536 CUDA cores, hailing from the firm's Ampere generation. Alongside the SoC, the handheld sports 12GB of LPDDR5X memory along with 256GB of storage.

Showing off the capabilities of these upgraded internals is a larger 7.9-inch, 1080p LCD display. It's larger and brighter than the original Switch display, but it also comes with far greater capabilities, including variable refresh rate, HDR10 support, and a 120 Hz panel.

The handheld is much larger overall, and it sports a redesigned Joy-Con mechanism that's far more resilient than the original Switch design. The kickstand more closely aligns with the Switch OLED, running along most of the edge of the display; the original Switch props up the console on one side, where it can easily fall over.

Overall, the performance jump is what stands out most. Nintendo has released several Switch 2 updates for flagship titles like Metroid Prime 4 and The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom with support for higher resolutions and frame rates. Titles like the recent Ocarina of Time Remake and Donkey Kong Bananza target 60 FPS, though occasionally fall short of that mark.

#10293. Samsung might copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island soon, but it actually looks good [Video]

A new leak suggests that Samsung is preparing to copy the iPhone’s Dynamic Island feature in the not-too-distant future, to an extent that not many other Android brands have even done thus far.

Copying the Dynamic Island was briefly like a rite of passage for some Android brands, with many wasting almost no time in delivering their own version of the feature. But, on many of those devices, it’s a surface-level ripoff at best. The feature is primarily limited to music apps and timers, rarely working with any third-party apps in meaningful ways. Android has a built-in feature similar to Apple’s but not in the same styling.

Samsung is now preparing its own version of the Dynamic Island, and it seems this one has a bit more meat on its bones.

SamMobile on Twitter/X posted a video and two still images of what appears to be a Dynamic Island clone coming to Galaxy devices. It’s mentioned as potentially coming to the Galaxy S27 series (so, One UI 9.5) and being called “Moment Effects.” A portion of the software explicitly mentions integration with Google Sports, the leak claims, while it seems Samsung is using Live Notifications here, which already support a number of third-party apps.

The kicker from a UX perspective is that Samsung’s interpretation allows you to swipe between activities, similar to Apple, but the core premise is similar with the island showing around the selfie camera cutout.

When this feature might surface does remain unclear, but a One UI 9.5 debut would make plenty of sense. That update is in beta now and due to launch on the Galaxy S27 series early next year.

What do you think of Samsung’s alleged Dynamic Island clone? Personally, I’m on board as long as it proves useful, and it seems to be on the right track compared to most other copycats.

#10289. 5 Things Most Developers Get Wrong When Turning Images into HTML

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Turning a design image into a working webpage can look simple.

You have a picture (a mockup, a screenshot, a Figma export), and your job is to turn it into working HTML in a browser.

But here is the catch. A design image is static, while a webpage is dynamic and responsive across different screen sizes and devices. A webpage needs to load quickly, adapt perfectly to different screen sizes, and allow touch interactions on mobile devices.

Most mistakes in this process happen when the conversion is treated as copying a picture instead of rebuilding it.

This section walks you through the mistakes developers often make, and more importantly, how to fix each of them.

Whether you are slicing a client mockup or converting a screenshot into code, avoiding these mistakes can save you from hours of rework. Let’s get into the details.

A screenshot reflects how a page appears at a particular display. But it doesn’t show how the layout will behave when the content changes or the screen gets smaller.

Developers sometimes try to copy the exact positions and dimensions of every element. To match the screenshot, they may use fixed widths, large margins, and absolute positioning.

This can work at one screen size, but the layout may break on smaller screens or when a heading wraps onto an extra line.

The better approach is identifying the layout rules behind the design. CSS Grid can arrange the cards in columns. Flexbox can help align elements within each card. And media queries can adjust the layout for smaller screens.

Pixel-level comparison can be useful in some cases, but it should not come at the cost of a flexible layout.

When developers recreate a design, it’s easier for them to focus on shapes and spacing rather than the purpose of each element. So, they may use generic elements for headings, buttons, navigation areas, and content sections instead of choosing elements that reflect each element's purpose.

This may produce the right appearance, but it often makes the code harder to maintain and understand. And when developers fail to use the right HTML elements, it can also create accessibility problems later.

So don’t put everything inside several unnamed containers. Instead, use a clear structure with appropriate headings, meaningful alt text for informative images, and real buttons for actions.

Semantic elements (e.g., header, nav, main, section, and footer) can also help organize a large page when they match the content’s purpose.

A design image may have headings, descriptions, prices, labels, or button text.

Reusing the entire image to reproduce the original design feels quick. But it can create problems when that text has to work as part of a responsive web page.

For example, there’s a promotional banner that contains a heading, a short description, and a CTA button. If you use the entire banner as one image, you can’t easily change the heading, translate the text, or make the button work as a real element.

So, don’t treat the image like that. Instead, use the original image where appropriate and recreate the text and button in HTML. CSS can control their position, size, color, and spacing.

Make sure you distinguish between visual elements that should remain images and text that should be recreated as HTML.

Not every part of turning images into HTML needs to be done manually.

Developers sometimes spend too much time on recreating basic elements when there are free online conversion tools available. These tools can help them get an initial version much faster.

An online conversion tool may be helpful when you need to extract text or generate a rough starting layout from a design image.

For example, you can upload a design image into an online image to html converter to get a starting point (rather than starting from a blank file). Review the tool’s output. Identify the parts you can reuse (as they are) and tweak the parts that need adjustments.

Keep in mind that the first result generated by the tool isn’t the final code. You may need to reorganize the HTML, refine the CSS, and adjust the layout to make it responsive.

Use automation where it saves time and your own judgement where it matters.

A screenshot shows just one view of a page. It doesn’t show how the layout may change on a phone or how people using a keyboard/screen reader can interact with it.

So, developers who focus only on the desktop design may end up with:

content that becomes too small on mobile devices

There’s also a chance of accessibility problems when images lack suitable alt text/interactive elements.

Let’s say a page has three cards in a row. On the desktop, this arrangement may work well. But on a phone, you may need to stack the cards vertically so their content remains readable.

That’s why accessibility should be considered during the same process. Use meaningful headings and provide appropriate alt text for images. Also, maintain sufficient text contrast and make sure interactive elements can be operated with a keyboard.

Lastly, don’t forget to test the page on both desktop and mobile screen sizes before finalizing it.

HTML and CSS handle the structure and appearance of a webpage, but JavaScript makes it possible to add interactive features.

For example, a design image may contain a navigation menu with a button. When recreating this design, developers should make sure the button performs an actual action rather than simply looking like a clickable element.

You can use JavaScript's event listeners to handle these interactions.

Now, add JavaScript to make the menu functional:

In this example, clicking the button opens or closes the navigation menu. The aria-expanded attribute also communicates the menu's current state to assistive technologies.

This is a simple example of why converting a design image into HTML involves more than matching its appearance. Developers also need to recreate the behavior of interactive elements using appropriate HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.

The process of turning an image into HTML feels easy when you treat it the right way.

Start by identifying the main sections, text, images, and interactive elements. Then create meaningful HTML and build the layout with CSS. Make the necessary adjustments to ensure it works across different screen sizes.

Use a conversion tool when it can really save time. But don’t forget to review the generated output of the tool thoroughly before using it.

A screenshot is a visual reference, not a complete set of development instructions. And the best result for you isn’t simply a page that looks like the image.

It is a page that also works well, adapts well, and stays easy to maintain.

#10288. Galaxy Z Fold 8 features: What you can do on a foldable

Folding phones have come in numerous variations over the years as the concept has evolved. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the latest evolution that introduces a form factor that fits every user. Its unique design makes it a foldable most people can actually use – which makes it a perfect gift. You might be surprised by what it can do.

It’s in the name. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 folds and unfolds like other foldable devices – that much you know. But what the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s form factor provides is something different to what most might be familiar with.

Instead of a tall device that unfolds into a near-uniform square display, you get a wider screen more like a tablet. That makes it perfect for watching videos or movies. Content that naturally works well on TVs or your computer will look great on the Galaxy Z Fold 8.

What about content that’s best viewed in portrait mode? Because it opens up to a tablet-like display, you can turn the Fold 8 to watch YouTube Shorts or Instagram Reels without a problem, so if you or someone you know likes to watch content on a bigger display, the Z Fold 8 makes that much easier. It really doesn’t matter what content you come across – the wider inner display gives you that large screen feeling.

Like a well-designed tool, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 has more purposes than the tasks best suited for the inner display. That shorter, wider profile makes the outer display smaller, but not as thin as something on other foldables, like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra.

In reality, the aspect ratio is similar enough across both the inner and outer screens that most content scales well on the shorter front panel. Social apps like X and Instagram look great because videos and images are able to fill the screen, rather than squeeze. Samsung’s One UI software also supports app optimization across both screens, although the experience may vary by app.

Saying the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s outer display is “minimal” isn’t a dig, either. Apps can be refreshing to use when you don’t need to open them on a large scale. Take Messages, for instance. It’s not a complex app, and there isn’t a ton to see – just the last couple of messages exchanged. The same goes for plenty of apps available on the Google Play Store.

And guess what? If you don’t like using the app on a smaller display, just unfold the phone. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is such a versatile phone, whether it’s a gift during the holidays or one for yourself.

There are instances where you need the large real estate. One UI 9 gives the Galaxy Z Fold 8 the option to open and run multiple apps on your screen. There are several ways you can do it, too.

The main approach is to split two apps on the wide inner display. But if you want to see more from one app, you can simply drag the split over in one direction. One UI 9 leaves room to add one more app to that split screen, giving you a three-way Split View. All it takes is dragging an app from the taskbar and choosing the spot.

If you truly need a fourth app open, you have the option to open another in a floating window. Having four apps open at once is definitely a niche instance, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8 handles it like it’s nothing. You can have YouTube up in one corner while you check Messages for gift ideas and shop through the Amazon app. It might be helpful to keep a list of what you got and for whom in a floating Sheets window.

The point is not only that it’s technically possible, but it’s usable. So often devices let users open apps in split screen, but are in no way comfortable enough for the task. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is perfect for it, and the experience actually makes you more productive, not less.

Most phones don’t let you get the best angle without any sort of third-party accessory. The Galaxy Z Fold 8, though, acts like its own tripod in a way.

Let’s say you’re taking photos, and you need to get a shot of yourself, but no one is around to take the photo for you. The Galaxy Z Fold 8’s hinge lets it prop itself up, so you can do things like set a timer and run to strike a pose.

This comes in handy for videos, too. Think of how many times you wanted to get a good angle on a plane or somewhere where there’s this flat surface just asking for a tiny tripod or kickstand. Well, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is its own kickstand and can be positioned at just the right angle.

You can get the Galaxy Z Fold 8 from your favorite carriers and retailers, but Best Buy is one of the leading places to find deals during the holiday season. You’ll find hefty discounts, higher trade-in values for your old devices, and more.

Windows 7 gets a second wind with modern GPU support.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Windows 7 may have been retired years ago, but that hasn’t stopped enthusiasts from finding ways to bring modern hardware and features to Microsoft’s beloved operating system. A new community project is now giving Windows 7 the ability to support modern Nvidia, AMD, and Intel graphics cards, along with DirectX 12 and Vulkan 1.4 capabilities.

Developed by enthusiasts EAZY BLACK, kryptik, and VisualEhrmanntraut, the project backports WDDM 2.2 from Windows 10. Interestingly, Windows 7 originally launched with WDDM 1.1, while Microsoft introduced WDDM 2.0 with Windows 10 in 2015 and WDDM 2.2 with the Creators Update in 2017. Microsoft also introduced a limited version of DirectX 12 for Windows 7 in 2019; however, that implementation was only meant for developers to port select games to the older operating system.

According to a post by @TheBobPony on X, Nvidia's RTX 40 and RTX 50 series, AMD's Radeon RX 7000 and RX 9000 series, and select Intel Arc graphics cards are supported. Screenshots confirm that the backport enables GPUs to be recognized by Windows 7 as well as third-party apps like GPU-Z. It is also claimed that features such as ray tracing and DLSS also work on compatible hardware. Older GPUs, including Nvidia's RTX 30 series and AMD's Radeon RX 6000 series, may also benefit from the project. That said, actual feature support depends on the graphics card, driver implementation, and application.

For anyone who still prefers Windows 7 or relies on older software and hardware, this project is worth exploring, especially if you intend to extend the usefulness of the operating system. Users can also combine it with the VxKex compatibility layer, allowing newer Windows apps to run on older operating systems. You can grab the project files and modified drivers by heading to the W7Revived website.

There’s a reason why enthusiasts continue to keep Windows 7 alive. Released in 2009 as a successor to Windows Vista, it offered a familiar interface, solid performance, and a straightforward desktop experience. It became one of Microsoft’s most popular operating systems and remained a favorite among PC enthusiasts and everyday users long after the arrival of Windows 10.

In fact, Windows 7 still has some life left in it. We recently tested the aging operating system against Windows 11 on a modern PC running on an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D, Radeon RX 6950 XT, and 64GB of DDR5 memory. The tests included eight DirectX 11 and two DirectX 9 titles, with Windows 7 delivering better performance in certain scenarios, particularly CPU-bound games, along with stronger 1% lows in some titles.

#10284. Gemini app rolls out very slick camera redesign on Android

Google has thoroughly redesigned Gemini for Android’s in-app camera that lets you quickly add an image to prompts.

Available from the ‘plus’ menu in the prompt box, the old interface launched a fullscreen viewfinder that was quite generic. This UI is still in use when you launch the camera from the Gemini overlay.

The new one appears inline on the Gemini homepage or inside a conversation. It launches as a bottom sheet that you can drag up — with a really slick animation — to take up most of the screen.

This nearly edge-to-edge redesign, which extends behind the system navigation bar, is a stark contrast to the old approach.

To the right of the capture button is a three-dot overflow button that animates on open, revealing options to control the flash and switch to the front-facing camera. There’s a back button on the other side, but you can also just swipe down.

This camera redesign first launched in the Gemini for iOS app, but has now widely rolled out on Android in both the beta and stable (version 17.76+) channels. Be sure to Force stop the Google app if you’re not seeing it live yet.

This follows the launch of GIF creation in the Gemini app yesterday. Powered by Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash, select GIF from the Format menu or specify “Create a GIF” in your prompt. Google frames this feature as an alternative to searching for GIFs.

#10281. Retail Merchandising Principles for Web Designers and Developers: Display Design, Ranking Rules and Measurement

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every e-commerce grid, product page and checkout screen is a shelf. Merchandisers have spent decades working out what to put at eye level, how many choices to show and how to tell whether a display works, and web teams are now rebuilding the same logic in code: sort orders, pinned products, badges, filters and A/B tests. Knowing where those ideas came from helps you build them with fewer mistakes.

This article maps physical retail merchandising onto digital product design. It covers visual hierarchy and choice load in listing UIs, a small rule-based ranking function you can test, accessible price and badge design with a contrast checker, and a significance test for layout experiments. It also covers why good plans fail at the handoff between design, engineering and operations. The numbers in the examples are illustrative, so use your own data. The code was run with Node.js 22.

Merchandising shapes how products are displayed, promoted and made accessible across sales channels. A well-planned strategy considers visual hierarchy, packaging, promotional materials, inventory timing and consistency between locations. For example, a skincare product may struggle to convert if testers are missing, shelf information is unclear or displays arrive after a campaign begins. If you build for the web, nearly every one of these problems has a direct counterpart:

Customers scan before they read. Nielsen Norman Group defines visual hierarchy as the arrangement of elements so the eye moves through them in order of importance, and names color and contrast, scale and grouping as the main tools. On a shelf, a larger facing or a contrasting background makes the hero product visible first. In a product grid, the equivalents are a larger card for a featured item, a clear boundary around a bundle, and a restrained use of badges so that only one or two items carry emphasis.

A common mistake is giving every card a badge, a color and a call to action. When everything is emphasized, nothing is, and the page reads like a crowded table in a store. Treat emphasis as a limited resource and have the design system say how many emphasized cards one screen may show.

A premium item placed beside entry-level alternatives may lose perceived value, and a crowded display can make fresh products look less appealing. Hick’s Law states that the time to make a decision increases with the number and complexity of choices, and its practical advice is to reduce options when speed matters and to highlight recommended ones. In a listing UI that suggests a curated first screen with a clear sort label, a few high-confidence recommendations and filters that handle the long tail, rather than an unbroken wall of products. Hick’s Law is a guide, not a rule, so treat it as a hypothesis to test on your own audience.

Merchandisers often ask for things like “pin the new range to the top, boost this collection, never show more than two items from one brand in a row, and push sold-out items down”. Implemented as scattered CSS order values and one-off queries, these requests become hard to audit. A small, pure function keeps the rules explicit and testable.

Because the function has no side effects, a short test can check every rule. This one confirms that pinned items lead, a pinned item that is out of stock is skipped, the brand cap pushes a third item from one brand behind other brands, sold-out items go last and a boost can lift a weaker product:

The design decision here is that stock demotion and the brand cap are defaults, while pins and boosts are inputs a merchandiser can change without a deploy, for example from a config file or admin screen. A real system would also log which rule moved each item, so that someone can answer why a product appears where it does.

Price cards and shelf labels are text, and text needs contrast. The WCAG contrast requirement asks for at least 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text. Product cards are a place where this slips, because grey secondary prices and pastel sale badges look refined in a mockup. The following script computes the WCAG contrast ratio, so you can run it over your design tokens in CI.

It prints 4.54 for mid grey #767676 on white, which just passes for normal text, 7.00 for #595959, which would also meet the stricter enhanced level, and 3.96 for the pink #d94f70, which fails for small badge text but would pass for large text. A sale badge that fails the check should be fixed in the token, not on one page.

A useful display respects the realities of the sales environment: floor space, customer movement, staff workload, replenishment access and how much information shoppers can absorb at a glance. The digital version is the gap between the design file and what ships. Test the setup with the people who will use it, not only with the team that created it, because their feedback reveals practical problems before a wide rollout.

A polished plan can fail in practice when materials arrive late, displays are assembled incorrectly or teams miss replenishment. On the web, the matching failures are a promotion that goes live before its product data is synced, a badge that stays on after a sale ends, and a hero banner that links to a discontinued item. Execution needs coordination across marketing, suppliers, logistics and engineering, with clear instructions, realistic schedules, display standards and checks. Some companies outsource this kind of field merchandising. One provider’s services page, for example, lists planogram and retail resets, end-cap displays, signage placement, on-shelf availability, in-store audits and visit reporting. Whether or not you hire help, that list is a useful checklist of what execution involves, and most items have a digital twin you can automate: availability checks, expiry dates on promotions and audit reports. Treat any performance figures on vendor pages as the vendor’s own claims and ask for evidence before relying on them.

Timing matters most for seasonal promotions and limited windows. A campaign that goes live after peak demand has passed cannot deliver the same value, so give promotional content a start and end date in the data model and have a scheduled job remove it, rather than relying on someone to remember.

Sales alone cannot tell you whether visibility, placement, stockouts, confusing information or poor setup caused a result. For a web listing, the answer is a controlled experiment. The script below compares conversion for a control grid and a variant with a two-proportion z-test, a standard way to check whether a difference is larger than chance would explain.

With these illustrative numbers, conversion rises from 3.00 percent to 3.90 percent, a relative lift of 30.0 percent, and the z value of 2.21 exceeds the 1.96 threshold for roughly 95 percent confidence. That does not prove the curated grid is better in general. It says this difference is unlikely to be noise for this test, and it assumes you fixed the sample size in advance and did not stop the moment the result looked good. Use a proper experimentation platform or a statistician for anything with real revenue at stake.

A strong merchandising operation does not require a large budget. A simple guide for product priorities, layout, pricing, replenishment and approved materials keeps execution consistent, and it is the physical version of a design system: a small set of approved components and rules that make the correct result the easy path. For a product card, that means a documented component with a fixed set of badge types, a defined maximum of emphasized items per screen and tokens that already pass the contrast check.

Consistency also protects trust. Damaged fixtures, inconsistent packaging, missing prices and outdated signs make good products look poorly managed, and the digital equivalents are broken images, stale prices and mismatched labels between the listing and the product page. Aim for consistency, not identical pages: a mobile grid and a kiosk screen can differ, but they should share product priorities, visual identity and core messages.

Temporary and in-store selling spaces demand quick communication. Visitors move fast, attention is limited, and interfaces must be clear from a distance and forgiving of mistakes. If you build kiosk, tablet or point-of-sale software, these constraints shape the interface:

When results disappoint, audit the real experience before commissioning a redesign. Think like a first-time customer: is the offer clear, are premium items distinct and can the team maintain the page easily? Government small-business guidance offers concrete analogues. The Queensland Government’s retail display guidance recommends showing essential information and prices so customers can decide quickly, placing popular or profitable items at eye level, and refreshing window displays every two to four weeks. The Westminster City Council guide similarly links visual merchandising, layout and planned promotions to attracting customers and encouraging them to spend more time and money in a business.

Prioritize visibility, clarity, stock accuracy and consistent execution before investing in a full redesign. Small operational improvements can make a meaningful difference, especially when they remove obstacles that repeatedly interrupt the buying process.

The public guidance cited here was written for general small-business use, and UX principles such as visual hierarchy and Hick’s Law come from interface research, so they are useful analogies rather than guarantees. The code is a teaching sketch: it ignores personalization, pagination, caching, search relevance and many other concerns of a production ranking system. Results vary with category, audience and device, so test changes in your own setting and treat any benchmark as a starting point.

What is merchandising in e-commerce? It is how products are displayed, ordered, promoted and made accessible across a site or app, including layout, labels, badges, sort rules and inventory handling.

How does visual hierarchy apply to a product grid? The same tools apply: contrast, scale and grouping decide what the eye sees first and which products read as related.

What should I measure first? Start with conversion, add-to-cart rate and stock availability for the listing, then add average order value and revenue per visitor once the basics are tracked.

How do I know a layout test is reliable? Fix the sample size in advance, randomize users, run it long enough to cover weekly patterns and check significance, for example with the two-proportion test above.

Do small teams need a big design system? No. A short component guide with a few badge types, spacing rules and accessible color tokens is enough to keep pages consistent.

Merchandising is applied interface design with a long history. Clear hierarchy, a curated first screen, readable prices, explicit ranking rules and controlled experiments carry over directly from the shelf to the screen. For designers and developers, the practical lesson is that a good layout only counts if it ships correctly, stays current and is measured. Audit the basics, put the rules in code, test one change at a time and record what you learn.

#10280. The Google Home app is better than Nest alone ever was, mostly

The Nest app is officially dead, but it’s worth taking a step back and remembering that, while there are some rough edges, the Google Home app is a better smart home experience than Nest alone ever could have been.

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

The Nest app has been on life support for years now. Google has been migrating legacy products into the Home app for a while and the last few puzzle pieces came in Nest Protect support, the death of the earliest Nest Thermostats, and other assorted adjustments.

Realistically, even long-time Nest users have had no reason to continue using the app over Google Home.

Having been personally, deeply invested in Google’s vision for the smart home over the past decade, I’ll say that the Google Home app we have today is better than the Nest app ever was or ever could be. A huge part of that comes from Google Home’s ability to pull together devices from third-party ecosystems. Being able to control your entire smart home from a single app is how the smart home always should have been, and between Google’s work and the Matter standard, we’re finally able to enjoy that pretty seamlessly for the most part. Automations, Google’s actually-useful AI features, and more only sweeten the deal. I’m also a big fan of what the Home app has become from a UX perspective. While there are always rough edges and bugs to clean up, the customizable homepage and well-organized sections make for a good – not perfect, but good – experience for the end user.

That said, there is one thing that the Nest app’s limited scope benefitted from, and that’s a focus on making your own product great.

Google’s move to the Home app for Nest devices has been a 5-year run of trying to clean things up, improve on the app, and basically bring back the way things were in the Nest app as far as the first-party cameras and thermostats are concerned. It’s taken ages, but we’re finally up to speed, mostly. I still hate that you have to reset Nest Cams to change Wi-Fi when the Nest app just let you change it, and the Nest app’s settings were much less overwhelming, but I’d say the pros far outweigh the cons at this point.

The Nest app was good at its job, but the Google Home app is a worthy successor, finally, and now the Nest app can finally rest. I mostly think the same applies to Gemini vs Google Assistant, with the newer assistant a better overall experience, but it’s easier to poke holes in that argument.

#10277. Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 vs Watch 9: When is it right to spend more?

The Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is worth spending more on if you want longer battery life, a brighter outdoor display, and specialized features for trail running and diving. The Galaxy Watch 9 is the better fit if you want Samsung’s latest everyday health tools in a smaller, lighter watch.

Both, however, are fantastic smartwatches with limitless potential. They share a Snapdragon Wear Elite processor and many of Samsung’s newest wellness features, so deciding between them is really about figuring out your priorities when it comes to buying a wearable this holiday season. We’re here to help you figure that out.

As we covered in our Galaxy Watch 9 and Watch Ultra 2 comparison, both watches move to Qualcomm’s Snapdragon Wear Elite platform. Both also ship with Wear OS 7 and One UI 8 Watch.

That shared foundation includes useful health insights. Vitals brings overnight measurements together to establish your personal baseline, while Daily Cardio Load helps you understand training demands and recovery. Hearing monitoring can flag potentially unsafe noise levels, whether you’re at a concert or somewhere unexpectedly loud.

Both also get five years of Wear OS updates and security patches, making either a useful long-term partner for your Samsung device, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra or Galaxy Z Fold8.

The clearest differences are size, battery capacity, display brightness, and outdoor capabilities. Here’s how the lineup breaks down:

As you can tell, the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 is considerably bigger and heavier, but you get a lot more capability for that additional size.

The Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 makes its strongest case when your plans regularly take you beyond a desk, a gym, or a charger.

Samsung rates the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for up to 60 hours with the always-on display enabled, compared with up to 30 hours for the Watch 9, and that extra headroom is appealing when your day starts with a workout and finishes well into the evening.

Then there’s the screen. In our hands-on with both watches, the Ultra 2’s brightness stood out immediately, with a higher peak that gives it an advantage when checking workout information outdoors, even in the bright sun. And its titanium frame provides added durability.

The Ultra 2 also adds Trail Run tracking for elevation and terrain impact, route guidance using compatible GPX files, and Nutrition Alert hydration guidance. These are tangible reasons to upgrade if your usual run involves trails instead of pavement.

Diving is another differentiator. Samsung specifies recreational diving support to depths of up to 40 meters, with automatic tracking of depth, dive time and water temperature — a remarkable feat for a piece of technology that lives on your wrist.

The best choice becomes clearer when you picture your own routine:

Choose the Galaxy Watch 9 for everyday wellness, a lighter fit, and strong value. Choose the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 for longer adventures, more demanding outdoor activities, and greater freedom between charges.

If you’re replacing an older Galaxy Watch, both offer a compelling new generation of health insights and long-term support. Both last a long time and look absolutely awesome with running shorts or a business suit. But the Watch Ultra 2 can take you that extra mile, literally and figuratively, and may be worth spending more on during Best Buy’s Holiday Deals event.

#10273. What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

If you are curious to learn more about Pathways, you can take a look at the following resources:

How to handle user, connector, and agent auth in Gemini Enterprise with a custom UI

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

How to securely connect internal Cloud Run MCP servers to Gemini Enterprise

#10271. 5 AI Tools for Creating Audio-Led Music Videos: A Practical Workflow

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can open on the singer’s first line, cut to another image as the instruments answer, and return to the singer for the key line of the chorus. If that return depends on a convincing close-up, test it before building the rest of the video. Choose a phrase with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel. Those two sounds give you clear points to check against the recorded vocal.

Use the recorded vocal as the timing reference. AI tools can help build opening shots, instrumental cutaways, and singing performances. Compare MakeSong, HeyGen, and Hedra on the singing shots themselves, and try Freebeat for an audio-led visual draft.

For creators developing multimedia experiences, the workflow also connects with broader web technologies. SitePoint's guide to building better video experiences on the web explores some of the practical considerations involved in working with video content.

AISong provides an image-led way to build the visual world around a recorded track. It fits a performance video that uses atmosphere and supporting shots in addition to views of the singer.

AISong’s AI Music Video Generator combines the song with a set of image references for a multi-shot sequence. Use that feature for the opening, an instrumental cutaway, or an establishing scene that can support the performance footage. Check whether the setting and the singer’s close-up look like they belong in the same video.

When preparing these assets for a website or multimedia project, consistency between images and video clips is important. This is also relevant to the process of building an AI media pipeline from concept art to web video.

Portrait art, rehearsal stills, or images of the setting can give these supporting shots an established look. It is particularly useful when some parts of the song call for visuals that give the audience a break from the performer.

MakeSong can build a video around the recorded track and reference images. Its lip-sync option requires a real human face. Start with a clear frontal image and check the result against the original vocal.

MakeSong’s AI Music Video Generator accepts audio, images, and a written prompt. The product page also offers lip sync for eligible face references. Give the close-up a particular vocal phrase and reserve a wider shot for the next musical response. Check mouth timing and the performer’s appearance at the cut.

This approach works when a single visual brief needs to connect the performance with the supporting scenes. The artist should have an approved reference and a clear idea of when the singer should appear.

HeyGen is a performer-led music video option. It is particularly relevant when the same on-screen singer should remain recognizable through more than one setting or camera distance.

Its music video workflow accepts a song and supports singing performance. Use a close and a wider view to compare mouth timing and continuity on the same vocal phrase. Watch for changes in the face or outfit when the framing shifts.

Choose this route when viewers need to follow the same singer across several scenes. This makes more sense than a single avatar clip when the performer is the visual thread for the whole video.

Hedra can animate a portrait from an audio clip. Try a single line with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel to see where the mouth falls behind the recording.

A clear portrait and one clean line give you a controlled test. Inspect the start and end of the phrase, then check how the clip cuts to the next shot. When only one line is convincing, use that line and let the original edit carry the rest.

A short close-up or a fictional singer’s entrance in the chorus is a good use for this approach. It is a practical fit when you already have an edit and only need one additional vocal moment.

Freebeat offers an audio-to-video workflow with a performance-oriented mode. It gives artists another way to test a performance cut driven by an uploaded track.

Use its singing video route for an excerpt with an audible pause and a clear vocal re-entry. Compare whether the performance feels natural during the pause and whether the visual action resumes where the vocal does. Keep the same approved track for the comparison.

Try this early to compare a full performance treatment with a version built from shorter clips before settling on an edit.

Decide how much of the release the singer needs to carry. An image-led sequence can support the track around key lines, a performer-led workflow can keep the singer present through the full edit, and an avatar clip can cover a single important phrase.

For developers and designers working with audio and video on the web, the final production stage also deserves attention. Video formats, playback behavior, and audio synchronization can affect how the finished content performs across devices. SitePoint's tutorial on the Web Audio API provides useful background on handling audio in web applications.

Judge the hardest line first and use the original vocal as the reference at every cut. Once the performance works, build the supporting shots around it and prepare the final video for its intended platform.

#10265. Sizing GPU Memory for a RAG Service: KV Cache, Context and Concurrency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) app is usually drawn as a language model plus a vector database. When it goes live, the part that fails first is rarely the retrieval. It is GPU memory on the generation model, because a model that loads fine for one request can run out of memory when several users send long questions at once.

This article shows how to estimate that limit before you deploy, with a short script you can run, and then covers what else in the pipeline can slow responses down.

Serving a model takes more than its weights. The GPU also holds:

the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with the length of every active request

memory used by the inference runtime itself

SitePoint's guide to running a local LLM on 10GB of VRAM makes the same point for a single desktop GPU: the usable budget is the total VRAM minus overhead, and the KV cache grows with the context window, so the real model budget is smaller than the number on the box.

For a RAG service, the KV cache matters even more. Retrieved passages are pasted into the prompt, so every request carries a long context, and every concurrent request holds its own cache.

The cache size per token depends on the model's architecture: two tensors (keys and values) for every layer, for every KV head. This script turns those numbers into a rough limit on concurrent requests:

def kv_bytes_per_token(layers, kv_heads, head_dim, dtype_bytes=2):

# Keys and values, for every layer, for one token.

return 2 *layers *kv_heads *head_dim *dtype_bytes

def max_concurrent(vram_gb, weights_gb, ctx_tokens, per_token, reserve_gb=2.0):

budget = (vram_gb - weights_gb - reserve_gb) * GIB

return int(budget // (ctx_tokens * per_token))

per_token = kv_bytes_per_token(32, 8, 128)

print(per_token // 1024, "KiB per token")

n = max_concurrent(24, 16, ctx, per_token)

print(ctx, "tokens ->", n, "concurrent requests")

For a 24 GB GPU holding about 16 GB of fp16 weights with 2 GB kept in reserve, it prints:

The numbers are estimates. Real runtimes add their own overhead, and the architecture values come from the model's configuration file, so read them from the model you actually use. The pattern is what matters: doubling the context length halves the number of requests that fit. A RAG prompt that stuffs in ten retrieved chunks costs real capacity.

Serving runtimes do not simply crash when the cache is full. The vLLM documentation on optimization and tuning explains that when KV cache space is insufficient for all batched requests, the engine preempts some of them and recomputes them later, which can hurt end-to-end latency. Its suggested remedies include giving the cache more memory, reducing the number of concurrent sequences or batched tokens, or sharding the model across GPUs.

So the symptom of undersizing is often not an error. It is slow, uneven responses under load. Test with realistic prompt lengths and several simultaneous users, not a single request.

Embedding and ingestion have a different profile from generation. Embedding is often lighter than serving a large language model, and some teams run it on CPU. Document parsing, chunking and tokenization also run on CPU, so a fast GPU cannot make up for a slow CPU upstream. SitePoint's tutorial on a local RAG system for private documents walks through chunking, embeddings, vector storage and reranking, and is a good map of which stages run where.

RAM: vector indexes, document loaders and caches all live in system memory, so large collections need more of it.

Storage: model files, indexes, logs and backups add up. Fast storage shortens model loading and re-indexing.

Network: if the vector database, application server and model server sit in different places, latency adds to every request.

Two layouts are common. A single instance runs everything and is simplest for prototypes and internal tools. Separated services put ingestion and retrieval on smaller machines and keep the GPU for generation, which scales better as traffic grows.

Memory estimates only hold if the GPU memory is really yours. On a shared or time-sliced GPU, other workloads can take memory and compute away from your service, which makes capacity hard to plan. Hosting providers describe this in different terms, such as passthrough, vGPU, MIG and time-slicing. For a view of how one GPU hosting provider explains those allocation methods, GPU Mart's GPU virtualization guide compares them. It is a hosting company's page, so confirm any provider's claims about isolation and performance with your own load test.

Whatever provider you choose, ask these questions:

Is the physical GPU assigned to you exclusively, or partitioned or time-sliced?

Do you have root access to install your own drivers, CUDA libraries and containers?

Which minimum commitments, regions and support terms apply?

A small stack can run as a handful of containers: an API service for request handling and prompt assembly, an ingestion worker, a vector database and a model server. Keeping them separate lets you monitor each one's memory and CPU use and move the heavy one to bigger hardware without rewriting the rest.

For a service that stays up all month, a fixed monthly term is easier to forecast than hourly capacity. Hourly capacity suits testing quantization settings or processing a one-off document batch.

Is GPU memory always the bottleneck for RAG?

Not always. Generation usually hits memory first, but slow parsing, a small CPU or a distant vector database can dominate response time.

Does quantization solve the memory problem?

It shrinks the weights, but the KV cache still grows with context length and concurrency. Test with your real prompt sizes.

It depends on volume. Small workloads can run on CPU, while a large ingestion job may benefit from a GPU for a limited time.

Size a RAG service for the worst realistic moment, not the single-request demo. Estimate the KV cache from the model's architecture, remember that long retrieved contexts reduce how many requests fit, watch for preemption under load, keep CPU, RAM, storage and network in the plan, and find out whether the GPU is exclusive or shared. The script above is small, but it turns a vague worry about memory into numbers you can check.
