#10219. What to get right before you ship webhooks

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, and Cursor are all sending webhooks now, and all four built them on Standard Webhooks, the open spec written by Svix. Downloads of the spec's npm package went from 1.7 million a month in September 2025 to 220 million a month currently.

This may be boring to hear for the nth time, but the reason is agents. An individual person checks on a running job a few times. An agent told to watch for changes checks every resource, every minute, for as long as it runs, and almost every one of those requests comes back with nothing new. An agent that starts a job, polls until it finishes, and then starts the next one spends most of its time waiting: six steps with 15 seconds of polling each add 90 seconds, even when every operation is fast. Polling more often adds load, and polling less often adds delay, so there's not really a sweet spot.

An event-driven API gets rid of that trade-off. It tells consumers when something changed or finished, so they stop asking. That matters most for work that runs for minutes or hours, like batch inference, fine-tuning, and video generation, and many APIs still don't offer it. Of the 57 AI APIs with long-running jobs that Svix reviewed for its State of Webhooks 2027 report, 19 make you poll for the result.

Webhooks are how most APIs push those events, and you can get a v1 out pretty fast. Making it dependable is an infrastructure problem, which is a class of problem that requires more time by default. Before you ship webhooks, you need an append-only event log behind every delivery, a fixed event format, signed payloads, retries with backoff, event IDs receivers can deduplicate on, per-endpoint isolation, and delivery logs your customers can read. If you skip one of these things, a customer is just going to bring it up later.

Each section below covers one of them with adaptable code, and Standard Webhooks works with existing verification libraries in most languages.

If the request handler that changes the data also sends the webhook, a crash between the commit and the HTTP call loses the event, and nothing records that it should have gone out. A customer whose endpoint was down for longer than your retry window has no way to find out what they missed.

Both problems go away if you write each event to an append-only table, scoped per customer, in the same transaction as the change. Webhooks are delivered from that table, and the same table backs an HTTP endpoint customers can read from a cursor. Svix calls this design POKE (Publish Once, Kickoff Everywhere). Build the log first: a consumer who misses a webhook needs somewhere to resume from.

Every code path that changes state calls one helper inside its own transaction:

Pass the helper the transaction handle. An insert through the connection pool commits separately, so you can end up with the event and no change, or the change and no event.

The advisory lock prevents a subtler bug. Postgres assigns sequence values at insert time, but rows only become visible at commit. With two concurrent writers, a reader can see ID 102, store it as its cursor, and then never see ID 101 when the slower transaction commits. Holding a per-customer lock until commit makes each customer's events visible in ID order.

Reading the log is one indexed range query. The endpoint returns a cursor even when there are no new events, so a client can always store it and ask again later:

authenticate stands in for the middleware that already protects your API and sets req.customerId. That scoping stops one customer from reading another's events. A customer who was offline for a week asks for events after their last cursor and gets everything they missed, in order. Publish your retention period, since it limits how far back that works. The same log can also serve synthetic created events for existing records when a new integration needs to load its initial state.

Customers write handlers against your payloads, so you'll be supporting every field you ship.

Give each event an id, type, timestamp, and data. Use the log row's ID as the event ID so webhooks and the pull endpoint agree. Name types as a resource plus a past-tense action, like invoice.paid, and publish a catalog with an example payload for each type.

Emit an event for each state change a consumer would otherwise poll for. Deletions are the ones teams forget most. Long-running jobs need completion and failure events, such as export.completed and export.failed.

Adding a field is safe. Removing or renaming one breaks handlers, so ship a new type such as invoice.paid.v2 and keep sending the old one until customers have moved over.

Anyone who finds a customer's webhook URL can POST a fake invoice.paid to it. A signature lets the receiver confirm the request came from you, wasn't modified in transit, and isn't an old request being replayed.

Standard Webhooks signs the string id.timestamp.body with HMAC-SHA256, using a separate secret for each endpoint. Including the timestamp is what lets receivers reject old requests. Generate secrets with "whsec_" + crypto.randomBytes(24).toString("base64").

Send the result in three headers. The ID comes from the log and stays the same on every retry. The timestamp is the time of the current attempt, so each retry gets a new signature.

Receivers need the matching check, so put it in your docs:

The function takes the raw body because frameworks that parse JSON and serialize it again change the bytes, and the signature stops matching. It compares with crypto.timingSafeEqual, since === returns faster on an early mismatch and leaks timing information. It rejects requests more than five minutes old to block replays. The signature header is a space-separated list so that during a secret rotation you can send one signature per secret and the receiver accepts either.

That code shows how the scheme works. In production, use a Standard Webhooks library on both ends. It implements the same algorithm:

Receiving endpoints go down during deploys and outages, and they return errors when they're rate limited. If you only try once, each of those is a lost webhook.

Count any non-2xx response as a failure, redirects included. Following a redirect to an arbitrary host is a security problem in its own right. Count slow responses as failures too. The code below gives up after 15 seconds. Publish whatever limit you pick, because customer handlers have to respond within it.

These delays match the schedule Svix runs in production: eight attempts over a little more than 27 hours. The early retries cover restarts. The later ones give someone time to notice an outage and fix it. After each failure, pass the number of attempts so far to nextAttemptAt, and when it returns null, mark the delivery as failed. The event is still in the log, so once the customer fixes their server they can replay it or read it from the pull endpoint.

Store attempt state in a table next to the log. Timers in process memory disappear when the process restarts, and you don't need a message broker to avoid that.

Decide what happens to endpoints that never come back. Svix disables an endpoint after five days of continuous failure and emits an event so the sender can tell the customer. Pick your own threshold, notify the customer when you disable their endpoint, and let them turn it back on.

Retries mean some events arrive twice. Usually the receiver processed the event but timed out before you saw its response, so you sent it again. Senders accept this at-least-once behavior because the alternative is dropping events, and receivers handle the duplicates.

Receivers deduplicate on webhook-id, which doesn't change between retries. The insert that stores the event can also be the duplicate check:

webhook_inbox needs a text primary key id and a jsonb column payload. The handler verifies the signature, stores the event, and returns 204. A background worker processes the inbox, so the handler responds well within the sender's timeout. express.raw gives the route the unparsed body that verification needs.

Put this pattern in your docs with a note about ordering. Events in the log have a fixed order, but webhook deliveries don't always arrive in it. A retried invoice.created can land after invoice.paid. Receivers should keep whichever event has the newer updated_at, or read the pull endpoint when they need strict ordering.

With a shared worker pool, one customer's hanging endpoint can tie up every worker for the full timeout while other customers' events wait. Limit concurrent deliveries per endpoint, and slow down delivery to endpoints that keep failing.

Customer-supplied URLs are also an attack surface. Someone can register http://169.254.169.254/ or an internal hostname and get your servers to call your cloud metadata service or private network. This is server-side request forgery (SSRF). Resolve the hostname before connecting, reject private and loopback addresses, and don't follow redirects. The delivery code sets redirect: "manual" for this reason.

Without a self-serve view, every "did you send it?" question goes to an engineer who has to search logs.

Give customers a page to add and remove endpoints, choose event types, and rotate secrets. For each message, show every attempt with its timestamp, status code, and response body. The response body usually contains the customer's own error message. Add buttons to replay a failed message and to send a test event.

Expose the same operations in your API. Creating an endpoint and choosing its event types should take one authenticated request, because scripts and AI agents now set up a lot of integrations and can't click through a dashboard.

This is the biggest item here because it needs a UI, and it's the one teams most often leave out of the first release.

You can build all of this yourself, and many teams have. It comes down to the fact that it's cheap to manage delivery for 99% of your webhooks, but it's nontrivial to guarantee it for 100% of them. This is also ignoring the UX side such as offering the customer portal for your customers to manage their webhooks in.

Svix, which sponsored this article, handles webhook delivery. You keep the event log and decide what goes in it. A small relay reads new rows and passes each one to Svix:

Svix also hosts the debugging portal. This call returns a single-use link where the customer can manage endpoints, read delivery logs, and replay messages:

The free tier includes 50,000 messages a month and doesn't require a credit card.

Before the first customer registers an endpoint, you should be able to answer yes to each of these:

#10217. How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

Gemini 4 Hydrogen: a faster model for general purpose robotics

#10213. How to Add AI Facial Expression Editing to Your Video Pipeline (2026)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Product teams keep asking engineers for the same feature: “Can users fix the reaction in this clip without reshooting it?” It lands on the backlog as “add expression editing,” and it hides a lot of decisions.

This guide compares the realistic integration options in 2026, sketches a pipeline architecture, models costs, includes a small runnable job helper, and covers the consent and audit logging you need before changing a real person’s face. Vendor details change quickly, so check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Information about features, pricing, APIs and policies in this article was checked on November 10, 2026 and may change. Check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Performance transfer is great for animation, but someone has to record a new take for every change. If your promise is “fix the reaction in the footage you already have,” direct editing fits better.

DRAMA is an AI facial changer from SyncMonster. In the web app, users upload a video or image (MP4, MOV, JPG, PNG), pick a face, choose an emotion, and set intensity on a slider from subtle to strong. The app offers four core emotions (Happy, Sad, Angry, Excited) plus presets such as Surprised, Serious, Evil and Intimidating, with the extra presets on paid plans. It supports emotion blending and editing one face in a group shot without touching the others. SyncMonster states that edits keep the original resolution, lighting and details such as glasses and beards, which is a vendor claim you should test on your own footage.

For developers, SyncMonster publishes public API documentation for DRAMA. Here is what those docs state, as checked on November 10, 2026:

A few things to note honestly. The same generate endpoint also serves other SyncMonster features behind their own flags, so read the parameter table carefully. In the version we checked, the parameter table marks an audio input as required on generate, and the drama-only example includes one, so plan for that in your request builder. The emotion values exposed in the API are the four core emotions, and the extra app presets did not appear in the API docs. The docs describe polling only, with no webhooks mentioned, and they do not publish rate limits, an SDK, or a numeric maximum video duration (an error code exists for exceeding a duration limit).

Runway exposes Act-Two through its developer API as the character_performance endpoint. You send a character (image or video) and a driving performance video, and Act-Two transfers facial expression, and optionally body and hand motion, onto the character. Output is 24fps.

Runway’s API pricing guide lists Act-Two at 5 credits per second of output, with credits priced at $0.01 each, so $0.05 per second. Earlier documentation mentioned a 15-credit minimum per generation, and the current pricing page does not list one, so check for minimums before you budget short clips. The documentation also exposes an expression intensity setting to control how strongly the performance is applied.

The catch is the input requirement: every edit needs someone to act the new reaction. That suits character animation, but it is a heavy ask for a user who wants a slightly warmer smile in an interview clip. It also drives one character per generation.

LivePortrait is an open-source portrait animation framework from Kuaishou’s KwaiVGI group. It animates a source image or video using a driving video or a saved motion template, supports video-to-video portrait editing, and offers retargeting controls for eyes and lips.

The README sets up a Python 3.10 conda environment and expects Git and FFmpeg, with an NVIDIA GPU recommended. It also notes that Apple Silicon runs roughly 20x slower than an RTX 4090, and that Humans mode needs PYTORCH_ENABLE_MPS_FALLBACK=1 on a Mac. That matters if you were hoping to prototype on a MacBook and ship the same setup. A basic run adapted from the README looks like this:

Here -s is the source portrait (an image or video) and -d is the driving clip. Retargeting is exposed through additional options and the Gradio interface, so check the README for current names because they change between releases. Also read the repository’s license file before you plan any commercial use.

The Hugging Face Spaces demo is handy for experiments, but production means owning GPU provisioning, queueing, model updates and quality review. You still steer with a driving input rather than a “happier by 30%” control.

Rule of thumb: if expression editing is a feature rather than your product, buy. If you need on-prem processing or model-level changes, build on LivePortrait and budget for ops.

Whatever the backend, the pipeline has five stages.

Here is the shape of the worker loop. This is illustrative pseudocode only, not any vendor’s SDK or real endpoints:

The idempotency and backoff tips below are easy to get wrong, so here is a small vendor-neutral module. The key function hashes the input file together with the parameters, so a retry with the same input maps to the same job. The poller backs off exponentially up to a ceiling and gives up after a maximum wait. It uses only the Python standard library.

A short test confirms the behavior: the same input gives the same key regardless of parameter order, different parameters give a different key, and the poller returns once the state becomes done.

Credits are not a shared currency across vendors, so compare units first and money second.

For prototyping, the DRAMA web app has a free plan (60 credits, 1080p, up to 2 minutes of processing, 2 projects, watermarked output), which is enough to judge quality on your own sample footage before writing integration code. The docs we checked list API billing in credits but do not state a dollar price per API credit, so confirm that with SyncMonster before you finalize a pricing model. Do not assume web-app plan credits convert at the same rate as API credits.

Plan details are as listed by the vendor on November 10, 2026 and can change.

Changing a real person’s expression can change what viewers believe they felt, so treat this as a product requirement.

For a product feature that edits reactions in existing footage, the DRAMA API is the most direct hosted option among those compared here: face detection with thumbnails for selection, per-face edits, and emotion values you set in code, with no driving performance. Go in knowing its current limits: four emotion parameters in the API, polling instead of webhooks, and no published rate limits or SDK. Starting with the free plan on your own clips is a low-risk way to judge quality. Product details and API access for this AI expression changer are on SyncMonster’s site.

Choose the Runway API if your users want to animate characters from an acted performance. Choose LivePortrait if you need on-prem processing or model-level control and have the GPU and ops capacity. Leave Flawless DeepEditor to studio editorial teams, and Photoshop to still-image workflows.

Yes. DRAMA by SyncMonster has a hosted REST API for editing expressions in existing video using emotion values, and Runway offers Act-Two through its API for performance transfer. LivePortrait is open source and can be wrapped in your own service.

Yes, with a direct editing tool. The DRAMA API takes emotion values from 0 to 1 for happy, sad, excited and angry on a selected face. Runway Act-Two and LivePortrait both expect a driving performance or motion template.

Run face detection first, let the user pick a face, then send that face’s ID with the edit request. The DRAMA API returns a face ID and a thumbnail for each detected face for this purpose.

Per vendor docs as of November 10, 2026: the DRAMA API bills 0.5 credit per second for face detection and 1 credit per second for drama generation, while Runway’s Act-Two API bills 5 credits per second at $0.01 per credit. LivePortrait costs whatever your GPU time costs.

Yes, but its README notes Apple Silicon can be about 20x slower than an RTX 4090. An NVIDIA GPU is recommended for anything beyond experiments.

Not a public one. DeepEditor is an Avid Media Composer extension with access by application, aimed at film and TV editorial teams.

Log the requester, consent record, selected face, emotion and intensity, backend used, input and output hashes, credits used, and a timestamp, and keep the original file.
